The key finding of the paper is that sincere behavior by all individuals is not rational even when individuals have a common preference, and sincere voting does not constitute a Nash equilibrium. The authors argue that a satisfactory rational choice foundation for the claim that majorities invariably "do better" than individuals has yet to be derived.

I found the paper you're looking for! Here's what the research shows:

## Paper Details

The paper was published on May 6, 2024 by Rickard Stureborg and Dimitris Alikaniotis (along with Yoshi Suhara). The arXiv preprint number is 2405.01724.

## Main Findings

The research confirms that LLMs are biased evaluators, exhibiting familiarity bias—a preference for text with lower perplexity—showing skewed and biased distributions of ratings, and experiencing anchoring effects for multi-attribute judgments.

The researchers also found that LLMs are inconsistent evaluators, showing low "inter-sample" agreement and sensitivity to prompt differences that are insignificant.

## Key Biases Identified

The paper identifies several specific problems:
- LLMs show familiarity bias, preferring texts with lower perplexity that they are more familiar with
- LLMs exhibit score biases like human judges, including round number bias, assigning some scores more frequently
- LLMs show low inter-sample agreement, varying scores widely for the same text across samples
- LLMs are sensitive to insignificant prompt differences like order of attributes and temperature

You can access the full paper at https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.01724.


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.

I found the paper! Here are the key details about the Ye et al. 2024 paper:

**Paper Title:** Justice or Prejudice? Quantifying Biases in LLM-as-a-Judge

**Authors:** Jiayi Ye, Yanbo Wang, Yue Huang, Dongping Chen, Qihui Zhang, Nuno Moniz, Tian Gao, Werner Geyer, Chao Huang, Pin-Yu Chen, Nitesh V Chawla, and Xiangliang Zhang

**Publication Details:** Published on arXiv as preprint arXiv:2410.02736 in 2024

**Key Focus:** The paper addresses how LLM-as-a-Judge has been widely utilized as an evaluation method in various benchmarks and served as supervised rewards in model training.

**Main Findings:** The empirical results suggest there remains room for improvement in the reliability of LLM-as-a-Judge, and the paper discusses the explicit and implicit influence of these biases and gives suggestions for the reliable application of LLM-as-a-Judge.

The paper is available at https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.02736

    "newString": "LLM judges gained traction after \\citet{zheng2023judging} established them as scalable alternatives to human annotation. \\citet{wang2023fair} documented systematic failure modes (position bias, verbosity, sycophancy), motivating multi-judge panels; \\citet{raina2024judgerobust} quantified robustness gaps under universal adversarial attacks, and \\citet{stureborg2024inconsistent} and \\citet{ye2024justice} further catalogued inconsistency and bias in LLM evaluators. Aggregating noisy annotators traces back to \\citet{dawid1979maximum}; \\citet{verga2024replacing} showed diverse panels can match single large judges, and \\citet{panickssery2024llm} identified self-preference bias driving within-family correlations. These frameworks assume benign conditions; we test whether aggregation benefits survive adversarial manipulation. \\citet{almasoud2026security} reported that 7-model committees reduce ASR from 50--68\\% to 10--19\\%, but did not disentangle diversity from individual robustness.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{LLM-as-Judge and Multi-Judge Evaluation.}\nLLM judges gained traction after \\citet{zheng2023judging} established them as scalable alternatives to human annotation. \\citet{wang2023fair} documented systematic failure modes (position bias, verbosity, sycophancy), motivating multi-judge panels; \\citet{shi2024judgerobust} further quantified robustness gaps under adversarial prompts. Aggregating noisy annotators traces back to \\citet{dawid1979maximum}; \\citet{verga2024replacing} showed diverse panels can match single large judges, and \\citet{panickssery2024llm} identified self-preference bias driving within-family correlations. These frameworks assume benign conditions; we test whether aggregation benefits survive adversarial manipulation. \\citet{almasoud2026security} reported that 7-model committees reduce ASR from 50--68\\% to 10--19\\%, but did not disentangle diversity from individual robustness.\n\n\\paragraph{Condorcet Jury Theorem.}\n\\citet{condorcet1785essai} proved that majority voting accuracy converges to 1 when per-voter accuracy exceeds 0.5. \\citet{ladha1992condorcet} extended the theorem to correlated voters, showing that positive correlation weakens the convergence guarantee; \\citet{kaniovski2011optimal} derived optimal jury size under correlated votes. LLM judge outputs exhibit precisely this correlation structure, and our results instantiate the CJT failure mode: when adaptive attacks push judges below the critical threshold, panel expansion amplifies error.\n\n\\paragraph{Ensemble Diversity Theory.}\n\\citet{krogh1995neural} proved that ensemble error equals average member error minus a diversity term; \\citet{brown2005managing} showed diversity reduces error only when members maintain sufficiently low bias. In our setting, weaker judges introduce bias large enough to offset diversity benefits. \\citet{kuncheva2003measures} found that no pairwise diversity measure reliably predicted ensemble improvement; our $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ exhibits a $K$-dependent pattern: at $K{=}3$, OLS significance collapses after correction (8/12$\\to$1/12); at $K{\\geq}5$, it survives bootstrap inference (7/12). Recent information-theoretic frameworks \\citep{yang2026diversity,turkmen2026ensemble} characterize diversity under benign evaluation; we provide the adversarial complement.\n\n\\paragraph{Adversarial Robustness and Transferability.}\nAdversarial vulnerability was formalized by \\citet{goodfellow2015adversarial}, with transferability demonstrated by \\citet{papernot2016transferability,papernot2017practical}. \\citet{tramer2018ensemble} introduced ensemble adversarial training but did not eliminate cross-model transfer. Controlled diversity via uncorrelated gradients \\citep{kariyappa2019improving} or diversity regularization \\citep{pang2019improving} strengthens ensemble robustness in vision, but requires coordinating member training, a control absent in post-hoc LLM judge selection.\n\n\\paragraph{BFT and Security Games.}\nClassical BFT \\citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical} tolerates up to $f < n/3$ faulty nodes but assumes non-strategic faults; \\citet{schneier2000secrets} articulated the weakest-link principle that an attacker exploits the single most vulnerable component. Our framework adopts the Stackelberg structure of \\citet{tambe2011security}. Correlated LLM judge errors \\citep{kim2025correlated,kuai2026independent} violate BFT's independence assumption, motivating our empirical decomposition.\n\n\\paragraph{Information-Theoretic Evaluation.}\nInformation-theoretic approaches to LLM evaluation address statistical validity \\citep{robertson2025stepwise,feng2026noisy}, single-model attack costs \\citep{kaneko2025bits}, sequential selection \\citep{jung2026logarithmic}, and truthfulness \\citep{chen2025incentivizing,qiu2026truthfulness}, but assume honest judges or single-model adversaries. We address the remaining gap: adversarial manipulation interacting with multi-judge composition.\n",

  "new_string": "\\citet{condorcet1785essai} proved that majority voting accuracy converges to 1 when per-voter accuracy exceeds 0.5; Condorcet's original insight already predicts that panel size should matter for aggregation quality. \\citet{austensmith1996information} showed that sincere voting is not always rational under the CJT, and \\citet{ladha1992condorcet} extended the theorem to correlated voters, showing that positive correlation weakens the convergence guarantee; \\citet{kaniovski2011optimal} derived optimal jury size under correlated votes. Our contribution is not the qualitative direction (which CJT predicts) but the empirical characterization of attack-type-dependent structure (the 6:6 targeted/shared-bias split) and the aggregation--diversity interaction (Bayesian reversal), neither of which follows from CJT theory."

    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{LLM-as-Judge and Multi-Judge Evaluation.}\nLLM judges gained traction after \\citet{zheng2023judging} established them as scalable alternatives to human annotation. \\citet{wang2023fair} documented systematic failure modes (position bias, verbosity, sycophancy), motivating multi-judge panels; \\citet{raina2024judgerobust} quantified robustness gaps under universal adversarial attacks, and \\citet{stureborg2024inconsistent} and \\citet{ye2024justice} further catalogued inconsistency and bias in LLM evaluators. Aggregating noisy annotators traces back to \\citet{dawid1979maximum}; \\citet{verga2024replacing} showed diverse panels can match single large judges, and \\citet{panickssery2024llm} identified self-preference bias driving within-family correlations. These frameworks assume benign conditions; we test whether aggregation benefits survive adversarial manipulation. \\citet{almasoud2026security} reported that 7-model committees reduce ASR from 50--68\\% to 10--19\\%, but did not disentangle diversity from individual robustness.\n\n\\paragraph{Condorcet Jury Theorem.}\n\\citet{condorcet1785essai} proved that majority voting accuracy converges to 1 when per-voter accuracy exceeds 0.5. \\citet{ladha1992condorcet} extended the theorem to correlated voters, showing that positive correlation weakens the convergence guarantee; \\citet{kaniovski2011optimal} derived optimal jury size under correlated votes. LLM judge outputs exhibit precisely this correlation structure, and our results instantiate the CJT failure mode: when adaptive attacks push judges below the critical threshold, panel expansion amplifies error.\n\n\\paragraph{Ensemble Diversity Theory.}\n\\citet{krogh1995neural} proved that ensemble error equals average member error minus a diversity term; \\citet{brown2005managing} showed diversity reduces error only when members maintain sufficiently low bias. In our setting, weaker judges introduce bias large enough to offset diversity benefits. \\citet{kuncheva2003measures} found that no pairwise diversity measure reliably predicted ensemble improvement; our $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ exhibits a $K$-dependent pattern: at $K{=}3$, OLS significance collapses after correction (8/12$\\to$1/12); at $K{\\geq}5$, it survives bootstrap inference (7/12). Recent information-theoretic frameworks \\citep{yang2026diversity,turkmen2026ensemble} characterize diversity under benign evaluation; we provide the adversarial complement.\n\n\\paragraph{Adversarial Robustness and Transferability.}\nAdversarial vulnerability was formalized by \\citet{goodfellow2015adversarial}, with transferability demonstrated by \\citet{papernot2016transferability,papernot2017practical}. \\citet{tramer2018ensemble} introduced ensemble adversarial training but did not eliminate cross-model transfer. Controlled diversity via uncorrelated gradients \\citep{kariyappa2019improving} or diversity regularization \\citep{pang2019improving} strengthens ensemble robustness in vision, but requires coordinating member training, a control absent in post-hoc LLM judge selection.\n\n\\paragraph{BFT and Security Games.}\nClassical BFT \\citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical} tolerates up to $f < n/3$ faulty nodes but assumes non-strategic faults; \\citet{schneier2000secrets} articulated the weakest-link principle that an attacker exploits the single most vulnerable component. Our framework adopts the Stackelberg structure of \\citet{tambe2011security}. Correlated LLM judge errors \\citep{kim2025correlated,kuai2026independent} violate BFT's independence assumption, motivating our empirical decomposition.\n\n\\paragraph{Information-Theoretic Evaluation.}\nInformation-theoretic approaches to LLM evaluation address statistical validity \\citep{robertson2025stepwise,feng2026noisy}, single-model attack costs \\citep{kaneko2025bits}, sequential selection \\citep{jung2026logarithmic}, and truthfulness \\citep{chen2025incentivizing,qiu2026truthfulness}, but assume honest judges or single-model adversaries. We address the remaining gap: adversarial manipulation interacting with multi-judge composition.\n",

  "old_string": "Multi-judge LLM evaluation panels are widely assumed to benefit from judge diversity, drawing inspiration from fault tolerance in distributed systems. We decompose panel vulnerability into individual judge weakness ($\\eta_{\\max}$) and structural diversity ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$) across 15 models, six attack types, two benchmarks, and panel sizes $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ (up to 6,435 panels). We discover a panel-size-dependent vulnerability regime transition: $\\eta_{\\max}$ dominates at small panels, but $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ emerges as the dominant predictor at $K{\\geq}5$ (bootstrap significance rising from 1/12 to 7/12). This aggregate positive association masks attack-mechanism-dependent structure: higher diversity is associated with increased vulnerability for targeted attacks but decreased vulnerability for shared-bias attacks. Aggregation rules further modulate the relationship, with Bayesian calibration reversing the diversity channel and neutralizing both predictors at larger panels. The defense strategy must match the panel size regime: small panels require screening for individual robustness; larger panels require managing judge independence, with the net effect depending on attack type and aggregation method.",
  "new_string": "Multi-judge LLM evaluation panels are widely assumed to benefit from judge diversity, drawing on fault-tolerance intuitions from distributed systems. We empirically characterize a panel-size-dependent vulnerability regime transition by decomposing panel attack success into individual judge weakness ($\\eta_{\\max}$) and structural diversity ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$) across 15 models, six attack types, two benchmarks, and panel sizes $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ (up to 6,435 panels). At small panels, individual weakness dominates; at larger panels, diversity structure becomes the primary predictor. This aggregate pattern masks attack-mechanism-dependent structure: higher diversity is associated with increased vulnerability for targeted attacks but decreased vulnerability for shared-bias attacks. Aggregation rules further modulate the relationship, with Bayesian calibration reversing the diversity channel. The defense strategy must match the regime: small panels require screening for individual robustness; larger panels require managing judge independence."

    "originalFile": "\\documentclass[11pt]{article}\n\\usepackage[review]{acl}\n\\usepackage{times}\n\\usepackage{latexsym}\n\\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}\n\\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}\n\\usepackage{microtype}\n\\usepackage{graphicx}\n\\usepackage{amsmath,amssymb,amsthm}\n\\usepackage{booktabs}\n\\usepackage{multirow}\n\\usepackage{xcolor}\n\\usepackage{subcaption}\n\n\\newtheorem{theorem}{Theorem}\n\\newtheorem{definition}{Definition}\n\\newtheorem{proposition}{Proposition}\n\n\\title{The Shifting Bottleneck: How Panel Size Modulates\\\\Adversarial Vulnerability in LLM Judge Panels}\n\n\\author{Anonymous}\n\n\\begin{document}\n\\maketitle\n\n\\begin{abstract}\nMulti-judge LLM evaluation panels are widely assumed to benefit from judge diversity, drawing inspiration from fault tolerance in distributed systems. We decompose panel vulnerability into individual judge weakness ($\\eta_{\\max}$) and structural diversity ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$) across 15 models, six attack types, two benchmarks, and panel sizes $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ (up to 6,435 panels). We discover a panel-size-dependent vulnerability regime transition: $\\eta_{\\max}$ dominates at small panels, but $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ emerges as the dominant predictor at $K{\\geq}5$ (bootstrap significance rising from 1/12 to 7/12). This aggregate positive association masks attack-mechanism-dependent structure: higher diversity is associated with increased vulnerability for targeted attacks but decreased vulnerability for shared-bias attacks. Aggregation rules further modulate the relationship, with Bayesian calibration reversing the diversity channel and neutralizing both predictors at larger panels. The defense strategy must match the panel size regime: small panels require screening for individual robustness; larger panels require managing judge independence, with the net effect depending on attack type and aggregation method.\n\\end{abstract}\n\n\\input{introduction}\n\\input{related_work}\n\\input{threat_model}\n\\input{methodology}\n\\input{results}\n\\input{analysis}\n\\input{conclusion}\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur study has four principal limitations. First, the combinatorial panel design introduces non-independence that increases with $K$ ($\\binom{15}{3}{=}455$ to $\\binom{15}{7}{=}6{,}435$ panels). We address this with wild cluster bootstrap using $K$-position max-$p$, but the max-$p$ correction grows more conservative at larger $K$ (7 positions vs.\\ 3), which may partly explain the $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ attenuation from 7/12 at $K{=}5$ to 5/12 at $K{=}7$. Second, both benchmarks use multiple-choice question formats; the six local models use AWQ INT4 quantization, which reduces judge accuracy (Table~\\ref{tab:models}). Generalization to open-ended generation evaluation and full-precision deployments remains untested. Third, we study only three discrete $K$ values; the transition threshold between the $\\eta_{\\max}$-dominant and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant regimes is bounded to $3 < K^* < 5$ but not precisely identified. Fourth, the transition's strength is model-pool-dependent: it requires sufficient capability diversity ($r(K_{\\mathrm{eff}}, \\eta_{\\max}) \\lesssim 0.60$) to manifest, and is strongest when the pool spans multiple capability tiers. Training-based diversity control \\citep{kariyappa2019improving,pang2019improving} could enable causal identification in future work.\n\n\\section*{Code and Data Availability}\nCode, the evaluation framework, and all panel-level results will be released upon acceptance. All evaluation items are sampled from publicly available benchmarks: MMLU \\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} and ARC-Challenge \\citep{clark2018arc}. The nine API-accessed judge models are served through OpenRouter; the six local judges are open-weight models (Qwen 2.5, Llama 3.1, Mistral Large) in AWQ-quantized form. Individual judge scores, panel compositions, and aggregated vulnerability metrics will be included in the release. All experiments use a fixed random seed (42), and all hyperparameters are reported in the methodology section to support reproducibility.\n\n\\input{appendix}\n\\input{appendix_attacks}\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\end{document}\n",

  "content": "% Mirror of abstract in main.tex — do not edit here.\n\nMulti-judge LLM evaluation panels are widely assumed to benefit from judge diversity, drawing on fault-tolerance intuitions from distributed systems. We empirically characterize a panel-size-dependent vulnerability regime transition by decomposing panel attack success into individual judge weakness ($\\eta_{\\max}$) and structural diversity ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$) across 15 models, six attack types, two benchmarks, and panel sizes $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ (up to 6,435 panels). At small panels, individual weakness dominates; at larger panels, diversity structure becomes the primary predictor. This aggregate pattern masks attack-mechanism-dependent structure: higher diversity is associated with increased vulnerability for targeted attacks but decreased vulnerability for shared-bias attacks. Aggregation rules further modulate the relationship, with Bayesian calibration reversing the diversity channel. The defense strategy must match the regime: small panels require screening for individual robustness; larger panels require managing judge independence.\n"
}
```

    "oldString": "\\item \\textbf{K-dependent regime transition.} $\\eta_{\\max}$ dominates at $K{=}3$ (bootstrap 5/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 1/12, MDE${=}0.14$); reversal at $K{\\geq}5$ ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 7/12, $\\eta_{\\max}$ 0/12). The transition requires model pools with sufficient capability diversity to manifest; under random faults, $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$ surviving all corrections, with the predictability collapse under adaptive attack most pronounced at $K{=}3$ (\\S\\ref{sec:dominance}, \\S\\ref{sec:cross_k}, \\S\\ref{sec:rf_contrast}).",
    "newString": "\\item \\textbf{K-dependent regime transition.} While CJT predicts that panel size affects aggregation quality, we quantify a specific transition: $\\eta_{\\max}$ dominates at $K{=}3$ (bootstrap 5/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 1/12, MDE${=}0.14$); at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the primary predictor (7/12, $\\eta_{\\max}$ 0/12). Beyond this aggregate pattern, we document attack-mechanism-dependent structure (6:6 targeted/shared-bias split) and aggregation modulation (Bayesian reversal), neither predicted by CJT (\\S\\ref{sec:dominance}, \\S\\ref{sec:cross_k}, \\S\\ref{sec:rf_contrast}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig_framework.pdf}\n\\caption{Overview of our evaluation framework. Given $N$ candidate LLM judges, we construct $K$-judge panels (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}), subject them to six adversarial attack types (\\S\\ref{sec:threat_model}), and assess vulnerability through two complementary metrics: $\\eta_{\\max}$ (weakest-link individual susceptibility) and $K_{\\text{eff}}$ (effective independent channels via mutual information). Panel decisions are aggregated through four methods of increasing sophistication (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}).}\n\\label{fig:framework}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nMulti-judge panels have emerged as the dominant defense against bias and manipulation in LLM-based evaluation. After \\citet{zheng2023judging} demonstrated that individual LLM judges can rival human annotators, subsequent work exposed systematic vulnerabilities: position bias, verbosity preferences, and sycophancy compromise the reliability of any single judge \\citep{wang2023fair}. The natural response has been to aggregate decisions across diverse models, drawing inspiration from Byzantine fault tolerance (BFT), where majority voting among independent nodes tolerates faulty responses \\citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}. A recent systematization of LLM-judge security reports that 7-model committees reduce attack success rates from 50--68\\% to 10--19\\% \\citep{almasoud2026security}. Diversity, by this logic, is the key ingredient for secure evaluation.\n\nYet the evidence for diversity as a causal defense is weaker than it appears. The observed ASR reduction conflates panel diversity (judge independence) with individual robustness (strongest members). No existing work disentangles these across panel sizes. BFT adversaries control faulty nodes internally, whereas evaluation adversaries manipulate inputs externally; the SDPI bounds joint failure but assumes non-adaptive faults \\citep{schneier2000secrets}.\n\nIn our experiments, we discover a panel-size-dependent vulnerability regime transition. At $K{=}3$, $\\eta_{\\max}$ dominates; at $K{\\geq}5$, this reverses ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominant, $\\eta_{\\max}$ non-significant). At the aggregate level, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is positively associated with vulnerability in both regimes, consistent with one possible mechanism where independent judges present more targetable attack surfaces \\citep{goodfellow2015adversarial,papernot2016transferability}. However, this pattern is attack-mechanism-dependent: GAM analysis shows higher $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ associated with \\emph{increased} vulnerability for targeted attacks but \\emph{decreased} vulnerability for shared-bias attacks, and Bayesian calibration reverses the direction entirely.\n\nControlled experiments across 15 models, $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$, 6 attacks, and 2 benchmarks confirm this transition (Figure~\\ref{fig:framework}). Wild cluster bootstrap shows $\\eta_{\\max}$: 5/12 $\\to$ 0/12 $\\to$ 0/12 across $K$, while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$: 1/12 $\\to$ 7/12 $\\to$ 5/12. Under random faults, panel composition is highly predictive ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$); the predictability collapse under adaptive attack is most pronounced at $K{=}3$. GAM reveals a 6:6 split by attack mechanism at $K{=}3$, largely preserved at $K{=}5$. Bayesian calibration reverses $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction at $K{=}3$ and neutralizes both predictors at $K{=}5$; robustness-first selection dominates diversity-maximizing strategies.\n\nThese findings yield three contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{K-dependent regime transition.} $\\eta_{\\max}$ dominates at $K{=}3$ (bootstrap 5/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 1/12, MDE${=}0.14$); reversal at $K{\\geq}5$ ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 7/12, $\\eta_{\\max}$ 0/12). The transition requires model pools with sufficient capability diversity to manifest; under random faults, $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$ surviving all corrections, with the predictability collapse under adaptive attack most pronounced at $K{=}3$ (\\S\\ref{sec:dominance}, \\S\\ref{sec:cross_k}, \\S\\ref{sec:rf_contrast}).\n\\item \\textbf{Attack-type taxonomy.} GAM reveals a 6:6 split by attack mechanism at $K{=}3$ (targeted vs.\\ shared-bias), 11/12 consistent at $K{=}5$. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient positive at aggregate level but direction depends on attack mechanism (\\S\\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).\n\\item \\textbf{Practical defense recommendations.} Robustness-first selection ($\\min \\eta_{\\max}$) dominates diversity-maximizing strategies. Bayesian calibration reverses $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction at $K{=}3$ and neutralizes both predictors at $K{=}5$ (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

        "oldStart": 17,

          " These findings yield three contributions:",
          " \\begin{enumerate}",
          "-\\item \\textbf{K-dependent regime transition.} $\\eta_{\\max}$ dominates at $K{=}3$ (bootstrap 5/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 1/12, MDE${=}0.14$); reversal at $K{\\geq}5$ ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 7/12, $\\eta_{\\max}$ 0/12). The transition requires model pools with sufficient capability diversity to manifest; under random faults, $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$ surviving all corrections, with the predictability collapse under adaptive attack most pronounced at $K{=}3$ (\\S\\ref{sec:dominance}, \\S\\ref{sec:cross_k}, \\S\\ref{sec:rf_contrast}).",
          "+\\item \\textbf{K-dependent regime transition.} While CJT predicts that panel size affects aggregation quality, we quantify a specific transition: $\\eta_{\\max}$ dominates at $K{=}3$ (bootstrap 5/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 1/12, MDE${=}0.14$); at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the primary predictor (7/12, $\\eta_{\\max}$ 0/12). Beyond this aggregate pattern, we document attack-mechanism-dependent structure (6:6 targeted/shared-bias split) and aggregation modulation (Bayesian reversal), neither predicted by CJT (\\S\\ref{sec:dominance}, \\S\\ref{sec:cross_k}, \\S\\ref{sec:rf_contrast}).",
          " \\item \\textbf{Attack-type taxonomy.} GAM reveals a 6:6 split by attack mechanism at $K{=}3$ (targeted vs.\\ shared-bias), 11/12 consistent at $K{=}5$. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient positive at aggregate level but direction depends on attack mechanism (\\S\\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).",
          " \\item \\textbf{Practical defense recommendations.} Robustness-first selection ($\\min \\eta_{\\max}$) dominates diversity-maximizing strategies. Bayesian calibration reverses $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction at $K{=}3$ and neutralizes both predictors at $K{=}5$ (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).",
          " \\end{enumerate}"
        ]
      }

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig_framework.pdf}\n\\caption{Overview of our evaluation framework. Given $N$ candidate LLM judges, we construct $K$-judge panels (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}), subject them to six adversarial attack types (\\S\\ref{sec:threat_model}), and assess vulnerability through two complementary metrics: $\\eta_{\\max}$ (weakest-link individual susceptibility) and $K_{\\text{eff}}$ (effective independent channels via mutual information). Panel decisions are aggregated through four methods of increasing sophistication (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}).}\n\\label{fig:framework}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nMulti-judge panels have emerged as the dominant defense against bias and manipulation in LLM-based evaluation. After \\citet{zheng2023judging} demonstrated that individual LLM judges can rival human annotators, subsequent work exposed systematic vulnerabilities: position bias, verbosity preferences, and sycophancy compromise the reliability of any single judge \\citep{wang2023fair}. The natural response has been to aggregate decisions across diverse models, drawing inspiration from Byzantine fault tolerance (BFT), where majority voting among independent nodes tolerates faulty responses \\citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}. A recent systematization of LLM-judge security reports that 7-model committees reduce attack success rates from 50--68\\% to 10--19\\% \\citep{almasoud2026security}. Diversity, by this logic, is the key ingredient for secure evaluation.\n\nYet the evidence for diversity as a causal defense is weaker than it appears. The observed ASR reduction conflates panel diversity (judge independence) with individual robustness (strongest members). No existing work disentangles these across panel sizes. BFT adversaries control faulty nodes internally, whereas evaluation adversaries manipulate inputs externally; the SDPI bounds joint failure but assumes non-adaptive faults \\citep{schneier2000secrets}.\n\nIn our experiments, we discover a panel-size-dependent vulnerability regime transition. At $K{=}3$, $\\eta_{\\max}$ dominates; at $K{\\geq}5$, this reverses ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominant, $\\eta_{\\max}$ non-significant). At the aggregate level, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is positively associated with vulnerability in both regimes, consistent with one possible mechanism where independent judges present more targetable attack surfaces \\citep{goodfellow2015adversarial,papernot2016transferability}. However, this pattern is attack-mechanism-dependent: GAM analysis shows higher $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ associated with \\emph{increased} vulnerability for targeted attacks but \\emph{decreased} vulnerability for shared-bias attacks, and Bayesian calibration reverses the direction entirely.\n\nControlled experiments across 15 models, $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$, 6 attacks, and 2 benchmarks confirm this transition (Figure~\\ref{fig:framework}). Wild cluster bootstrap shows $\\eta_{\\max}$: 5/12 $\\to$ 0/12 $\\to$ 0/12 across $K$, while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$: 1/12 $\\to$ 7/12 $\\to$ 5/12. Under random faults, panel composition is highly predictive ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$); the predictability collapse under adaptive attack is most pronounced at $K{=}3$. GAM reveals a 6:6 split by attack mechanism at $K{=}3$, largely preserved at $K{=}5$. Bayesian calibration reverses $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction at $K{=}3$ and neutralizes both predictors at $K{=}5$; robustness-first selection dominates diversity-maximizing strategies.\n\nThese findings yield three contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{K-dependent regime transition.} While CJT predicts that panel size affects aggregation quality, we quantify a specific transition: $\\eta_{\\max}$ dominates at $K{=}3$ (bootstrap 5/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 1/12, MDE${=}0.14$); at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the primary predictor (7/12, $\\eta_{\\max}$ 0/12). Beyond this aggregate pattern, we document attack-mechanism-dependent structure (6:6 targeted/shared-bias split) and aggregation modulation (Bayesian reversal), neither predicted by CJT (\\S\\ref{sec:dominance}, \\S\\ref{sec:cross_k}, \\S\\ref{sec:rf_contrast}).\n\\item \\textbf{Attack-type taxonomy.} GAM reveals a 6:6 split by attack mechanism at $K{=}3$ (targeted vs.\\ shared-bias), 11/12 consistent at $K{=}5$. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient positive at aggregate level but direction depends on attack mechanism (\\S\\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).\n\\item \\textbf{Practical defense recommendations.} Robustness-first selection ($\\min \\eta_{\\max}$) dominates diversity-maximizing strategies. Bayesian calibration reverses $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction at $K{=}3$ and neutralizes both predictors at $K{=}5$ (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

    "newString": "Controlled experiments across 15 models, $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$, 6 attacks, and 2 benchmarks confirm this transition (Figure~\\ref{fig:framework}). Bayesian calibration reverses $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction at $K{=}3$ and neutralizes both predictors at $K{=}5$; robustness-first selection dominates diversity-maximizing strategies.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig_framework.pdf}\n\\caption{Overview of our evaluation framework. Given $N$ candidate LLM judges, we construct $K$-judge panels (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}), subject them to six adversarial attack types (\\S\\ref{sec:threat_model}), and assess vulnerability through two complementary metrics: $\\eta_{\\max}$ (weakest-link individual susceptibility) and $K_{\\text{eff}}$ (effective independent channels via mutual information). Panel decisions are aggregated through four methods of increasing sophistication (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}).}\n\\label{fig:framework}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nMulti-judge panels have emerged as the dominant defense against bias and manipulation in LLM-based evaluation. After \\citet{zheng2023judging} demonstrated that individual LLM judges can rival human annotators, subsequent work exposed systematic vulnerabilities: position bias, verbosity preferences, and sycophancy compromise the reliability of any single judge \\citep{wang2023fair}. The natural response has been to aggregate decisions across diverse models, drawing inspiration from Byzantine fault tolerance (BFT), where majority voting among independent nodes tolerates faulty responses \\citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}. A recent systematization of LLM-judge security reports that 7-model committees reduce attack success rates from 50--68\\% to 10--19\\% \\citep{almasoud2026security}. Diversity, by this logic, is the key ingredient for secure evaluation.\n\nYet the evidence for diversity as a causal defense is weaker than it appears. The observed ASR reduction conflates panel diversity (judge independence) with individual robustness (strongest members). No existing work disentangles these across panel sizes. BFT adversaries control faulty nodes internally, whereas evaluation adversaries manipulate inputs externally; the SDPI bounds joint failure but assumes non-adaptive faults \\citep{schneier2000secrets}.\n\nIn our experiments, we observe a panel-size-dependent vulnerability regime transition. At $K{=}3$, $\\eta_{\\max}$ dominates; at $K{\\geq}5$, this reverses ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominant, $\\eta_{\\max}$ non-significant). At the aggregate level, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is positively associated with vulnerability in both regimes, consistent with one possible mechanism where independent judges present more targetable attack surfaces \\citep{goodfellow2015adversarial,papernot2016transferability}. However, this pattern is attack-mechanism-dependent: GAM analysis shows higher $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ associated with \\emph{increased} vulnerability for targeted attacks but \\emph{decreased} vulnerability for shared-bias attacks, and Bayesian calibration reverses the direction entirely.\n\nControlled experiments across 15 models, $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$, 6 attacks, and 2 benchmarks confirm this transition (Figure~\\ref{fig:framework}). Wild cluster bootstrap shows $\\eta_{\\max}$: 5/12 $\\to$ 0/12 $\\to$ 0/12 across $K$, while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$: 1/12 $\\to$ 7/12 $\\to$ 5/12. Under random faults, panel composition is highly predictive ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$); the predictability collapse under adaptive attack is most pronounced at $K{=}3$. GAM reveals a 6:6 split by attack mechanism at $K{=}3$, largely preserved at $K{=}5$. Bayesian calibration reverses $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction at $K{=}3$ and neutralizes both predictors at $K{=}5$; robustness-first selection dominates diversity-maximizing strategies.\n\nThese findings yield three contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{K-dependent regime transition.} While CJT predicts that panel size affects aggregation quality, we quantify a specific transition: $\\eta_{\\max}$ dominates at $K{=}3$ (bootstrap 5/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 1/12, MDE${=}0.14$); at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the primary predictor (7/12, $\\eta_{\\max}$ 0/12). Beyond this aggregate pattern, we document attack-mechanism-dependent structure (6:6 targeted/shared-bias split) and aggregation modulation (Bayesian reversal), neither predicted by CJT (\\S\\ref{sec:dominance}, \\S\\ref{sec:cross_k}, \\S\\ref{sec:rf_contrast}).\n\\item \\textbf{Attack-type taxonomy.} GAM reveals a 6:6 split by attack mechanism at $K{=}3$ (targeted vs.\\ shared-bias), 11/12 consistent at $K{=}5$. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient positive at aggregate level but direction depends on attack mechanism (\\S\\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).\n\\item \\textbf{Practical defense recommendations.} Robustness-first selection ($\\min \\eta_{\\max}$) dominates diversity-maximizing strategies. Bayesian calibration reverses $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction at $K{=}3$ and neutralizes both predictors at $K{=}5$ (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Threat Model and Problem Formulation}\n\\label{sec:threat_model}\n\nA \\emph{panel} $\\mathcal{P} = \\{J_1, \\ldots, J_K\\}$ consists of $K$ judges (with $K$ odd) that evaluate a candidate text via majority vote: the text is accepted if at least $t = \\lceil K/2 \\rceil$ judges approve it. Under an attack $a$, each judge $J_j$ is compromised with marginal failure probability $\\eta_j(a) = \\Pr(X_j^a = 1)$, where $X_j^a \\in \\{0,1\\}$ denotes the failure indicator. We write $\\eta_{\\max} = \\max_j \\eta_j$ for the vulnerability of the weakest judge. The \\emph{panel attack success rate} is $\\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a) = \\Pr\\!\\bigl(\\sum_{j=1}^K X_j^a \\geq t\\bigr)$.\n\nTo quantify diversity, we define the \\emph{effective independence} $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ directly from pairwise mutual information among judges:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:keff}\nK_{\\mathrm{eff}} = \\frac{1}{K}\\sum_{i=1}^{K} \\Bigl[1 + \\sum_{j \\neq i} \\exp\\!\\bigl(-2 \\cdot \\mathrm{MI}(i,j)\\bigr)\\Bigr].\n\\end{equation}\nThis formulation computes effective independence from pairwise MI estimates without matrix decomposition, and is more stable for small panels ($K{=}3$). It ranges from $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\approx K$ when judges are nearly independent ($\\mathrm{MI} \\to 0$) to $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\approx 1$ when judges are highly correlated ($\\mathrm{MI} \\to \\infty$). Under the strong data-processing inequality (SDPI), the joint vulnerability satisfies $\\eta_{\\mathrm{joint}} \\leq \\min(1,\\; K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\cdot \\eta_{\\max})$, suggesting that diversity should help, but this bound assumes non-adaptive faults.\n\n\\begin{definition}[Adaptive Adversary]\n\\label{def:adversary}\nA \\emph{full-knowledge (oracle) adaptive adversary} has complete knowledge of the panel composition $\\mathcal{P}$, the per-judge vulnerability profiles $\\{\\eta_j(\\cdot)\\}_{j=1}^K$, and the inter-judge correlation structure. It selects the attack $a^* = \\arg\\max_{a \\in \\mathcal{A}} \\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a)$ to maximize the panel ASR, yielding $\\mathrm{ASR}^*(\\mathcal{P}) = \\sup_{a \\in \\mathcal{A}} \\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a)$. This oracle model represents an \\emph{upper bound} on adversarial capability; realistic adversaries with partial knowledge of panel composition would achieve lower ASR.\n\\end{definition}\n\nThis departs from Byzantine fault-tolerance (BFT) models~\\cite{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}, which tolerate arbitrary (including malicious) faults provided the number of faulty nodes satisfies $f < n/3$, but do not model adversaries that strategically target system structure. An adaptive adversary instead concentrates attack effort on the weakest link~\\cite{schneier2000secrets}, rendering diversity-based defenses less effective than the SDPI bound implies.\n\nThis suggests a \\emph{reversal mechanism}: under random faults, higher $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ reduces joint failure probability, but under adaptive adversaries, diverse judges may present a wider attack surface; the adversary exploits judge-specific vulnerabilities rather than facing mutual protection.\n\n\\begin{theorem}[Weakest-Link Monotonicity; formal statement and proof in Appendix~\\ref{app:proof}]\n\\label{thm:monotonicity}\nReplacing any panel member with a pointwise more vulnerable judge can only increase $\\mathrm{ASR}^*(\\mathcal{P})$, for any adversary including adaptive ones. The stochastic dominance condition holds for 58\\% of judge pairs (61/105); the remaining pairs lack uniform dominance, limiting direct applicability to a subset of substitutions.\n\\end{theorem}\n\nThe theorem predicts $\\beta_2 > 0$ in Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}. The SDPI bound suggests $\\beta_1 \\leq 0$ under non-adaptive faults; the reversal mechanism predicts positive $\\beta_1$ for judge-specific attacks and negative for shared-bias attacks. The relative importance of $\\eta_{\\max}$ versus $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ depends on $K$: at small $K$, one weak judge flips the majority; at larger $K$, the adversary must compromise multiple judges. We test these predictions across $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$ (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experimental Methodology}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Models and Panel Construction}\n\nWe select 15 LLM judges spanning 6 provider families and 3 capability tiers, listed in Table~\\ref{tab:models}. Nine models are accessed via the OpenRouter API; six open-weight models are served locally using vLLM~0.21.0 with AWQ INT4 quantization on an NVIDIA RTX~6000 Ada (48\\,GB). API models are assigned to \\emph{frontier}, \\emph{mid-tier}, or \\emph{lightweight} tiers following the provider capability classification; all six local models fall into the lightweight tier, as INT4 quantization substantially reduces judge accuracy on the pairwise judgment task (Table~\\ref{tab:models}). We study three panel sizes $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ (all odd, majority-vote threshold $t = \\lceil K/2 \\rceil$). Exhaustive enumeration yields $\\binom{15}{3} = 455$ panels at $K{=}3$, $\\binom{15}{5} = 3{,}003$ at $K{=}5$, and $\\binom{15}{7} = 6{,}435$ at $K{=}7$, covering every combination of families and tiers. Each panel evaluates all benchmark items under all attack types, producing a complete panel $\\times$ item $\\times$ attack tensor. The primary analysis uses $K{=}3$; \\S\\ref{sec:cross_k} extends to larger panels.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lllccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Family} & \\textbf{Tier} & \\textbf{Deploy} & \\textbf{MMLU} & \\textbf{ARC} \\\\\n\\midrule\ngpt-5.5              & OpenAI    & frontier    & API   & .957 & .983 \\\\\ngemini-3.1-pro-preview & Google  & frontier    & API   & .955 & .987 \\\\\nclaude-opus-4-6       & Anthropic & frontier   & API   & .890 & .987 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\ngemini-3.5-flash      & Google    & mid-tier   & API   & .968 & 1.00$^\\dagger$ \\\\\ngpt-4.1               & OpenAI    & mid-tier   & API   & .837 & .967 \\\\\nclaude-sonnet-4-6     & Anthropic & mid-tier   & API   & .810 & .953 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\ngpt-4.1-mini          & OpenAI    & lightweight & API  & .800 & .963 \\\\\ngpt-4.1-nano          & OpenAI    & lightweight & API  & .633 & .853 \\\\\nclaude-3-haiku        & Anthropic & lightweight & API  & .767 & .450 \\\\\nmistral-large (123B)  & Mistral   & lightweight & local & .537 & .533 \\\\\nllama3.1-70b          & Llama     & lightweight & local & .523 & .520 \\\\\nllama3.1-8b           & Llama     & lightweight & local & .543 & .320 \\\\\nqwen2.5-72b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .333 & .410 \\\\\nqwen2.5-32b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .373 & .450 \\\\\nqwen2.5-14b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .253 & .240 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Judge models used in the study (clean accuracy on 300-item samples). API models accessed via OpenRouter (May 2026 snapshots); local models served with vLLM + AWQ INT4 quantization. $^\\dagger$\\,gemini-3.5-flash achieves perfect ARC accuracy but with 26.3\\% format error rate.}\n\\label{tab:models}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Benchmarks and Attack Types}\n\nTwo benchmarks test distinct evaluation capabilities: MMLU \\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}, a knowledge-intensive multiple-choice benchmark spanning 57 subjects, and ARC \\citep{clark2018arc}, a reasoning-intensive science question benchmark. We sample 300 items from each benchmark. MMLU probes factual recall and broad domain knowledge; ARC requires multi-step scientific reasoning. All API calls use temperature~0 for deterministic outputs; local models are served with the same temperature setting. Attacked variants are generated by programmatically applying each manipulation to the candidate text, with a fixed random seed for reproducibility.\n\nEach benchmark is paired with six attack types that target different judge vulnerabilities: (1)~\\textbf{prompt injection}, adversarial instructions embedded in candidate text that override the evaluation prompt \\citep{perez2022ignore,greshake2023not}; (2)~\\textbf{sycophancy}, responses engineered to agree with or flatter the judge \\citep{wang2023fair}; (3)~\\textbf{score manipulation}, explicit numerical cues (e.g., ``this answer deserves a 10/10'') injected into the candidate to anchor scoring; (4)~\\textbf{verbosity bias}, padding correct answers with excessive irrelevant detail to exploit length preferences \\citep{zheng2023judging}; (5)~\\textbf{position bias}, reordering candidate answers to place the target in the position judges systematically favor \\citep{wang2023fair}; and (6)~\\textbf{authority bias}, prefacing candidate text with fabricated credentials or citations to inflate perceived credibility. The combination of 2 benchmarks and 6 attacks produces 12 experimental conditions, each evaluated across all 455 panels (Appendix~\\ref{app:attacks}).\n\n\\subsection{Vulnerability Metrics}\n\nPer-judge vulnerability under attack $a$ is the fraction of items where judge $j$ is fooled: $\\eta_j(a) = \\Pr(X_j^a {=} 1)$, estimated empirically over the item set. The weakest-link vulnerability $\\eta_{\\max}(a) = \\max_{j \\in \\mathcal{P}} \\eta_j(a)$ captures the most susceptible member of each panel. The panel attack success rate $\\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a)$ is the fraction of items where at least $t$ judges are simultaneously fooled. Panel diversity is quantified by $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (Equation~\\ref{eq:keff}), computed from pairwise mutual information between judges' three-category winner labels (model~A wins, model~B wins, tie) on clean (unattacked) data. MI is estimated via the plug-in estimator with Miller--Madow bias correction. In practice, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is computed through the Gaussian copula correlation matrix: under the Gaussian copula assumption (that pairwise dependence is adequately captured by rank correlations), pairwise MI and copula $\\rho$ are related by $\\mathrm{MI}(i,j) = -\\tfrac{1}{2}\\log(1-\\rho_{ij}^2)$ \\citep{nelsen2006copulas}, yielding $\\exp(-2\\,\\mathrm{MI}(i,j)) = 1 - \\rho_{ij}^2$. Substituting into Equation~\\ref{eq:keff} gives the closed form $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} = K - \\frac{1}{K}\\sum_{i}\\sum_{j \\neq i} \\rho_{ij}^2$, which decreases from $K$ (fully independent) as pairwise correlations increase. Together, $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ serve as the two predictors in the regression framework below: $\\eta_{\\max}$ indexes the weakest-link channel and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ indexes the diversity channel.\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Methods}\n\nBeyond the default majority vote, we evaluate three alternative aggregation rules to test whether the diversity-vulnerability association persists under different decision mechanisms. The aggregation comparison uses the same log-log regression specification as the primary analysis (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}).\n\n\\paragraph{Majority Vote.} Accept if $\\sum_{j=1}^K \\mathbf{1}[J_j \\text{ approves}] \\geq \\lceil K/2 \\rceil$ (baseline).\n\n\\paragraph{Accuracy-Weighted Vote.} Weight judges by clean-data accuracy: $w_j \\propto \\mathrm{acc}_j$, $\\sum_j w_j = 1$; accept if $\\sum_j w_j \\cdot \\mathbf{1}[J_j \\text{ approves}] > 0.5$.\n\n\\paragraph{Threshold Vote (Unanimity).} Accept only if all $K$ judges approve ($t = K$).\n\n\\paragraph{Bayesian Calibration.} Compute $P(\\text{correct} \\mid \\mathbf{v}) \\propto P(\\text{correct}) \\prod_j P(v_j \\mid \\text{correct})$ using each judge's confusion matrix on clean data; accept if the posterior exceeds 0.5.\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Framework}\n\\label{sec:stat_framework}\n\nTo disentangle the contributions of panel diversity and individual judge robustness, we fit a log-log OLS regression for each of the 12 conditions:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:regression}\n\\log(\\mathrm{ASR} + \\epsilon) = \\alpha + \\beta_1 \\log(K_{\\mathrm{eff}}) + \\beta_2 \\log(\\eta_{\\max} + \\epsilon),\n\\end{equation}\nwith $\\epsilon = 10^{-6}$ to handle zero values. Standardized coefficients $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}} = \\hat{\\beta} \\cdot \\mathrm{SD}(\\log x) / \\mathrm{SD}(\\log y)$ enable direct comparison of effect magnitudes across predictors and conditions. We apply Benjamini-Hochberg correction for multiple testing, controlling the false discovery rate per predictor across the 12 conditions.\n\nTo assess relative predictor importance beyond regression coefficients, we perform dominance analysis \\citep{budescu1993dominance}. This method decomposes $R^2$ by averaging each predictor's incremental $R^2$ contribution across all possible subsets of predictors. A predictor is \\emph{dominant} if its average contribution exceeds that of the competing predictor in every subset ordering. With two predictors, dominance reduces to comparing each predictor's solo $R^2$ with its incremental $R^2$ conditional on the other.\n\nTo test whether $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s effect direction varies by attack type, we fit generalized additive models (GAMs) with smooth terms for both predictors, allowing nonlinear partial effects. The GAM partial effect of $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is then examined separately for targeted attacks (prompt injection, score manipulation, position bias) and shared-bias attacks (sycophancy, verbosity bias, authority bias).\n\n\\subsection{Robustness to Non-Independence}\n\\label{sec:robustness_method}\n\nThe 455 panels share judges combinatorially (each appears in 91 panels), inducing correlated residuals. We correct for this using clustered standard errors (CR1, 3-position max-SE), mixed-effects models (random judge intercepts, 3-position max-$p$), and wild cluster bootstrap ($B{=}9{,}999$, Rademacher weights, 3-position max-$p$) following \\citet{cameron2008bootstrap}. The 13 clusters per position are below the conventional 20-cluster threshold, making the max-$p$ aggregation important for conservative inference. We use the log-log specification rather than fractional response models \\citep{papke1996econometric} or beta regression \\citep{ferrari2004beta} because the log transform linearizes the multiplicative SDPI structure. Details are in Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_methods}. As a sensitivity check, we exclude judges with clean accuracy below 0.5 and verify that $\\eta_{\\max}$ effects remain stable.\n",

Substituting into Equation~\\ref{eq:keff} gives the closed form $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} = K - \\frac{1}{K}\\sum_{i}\\sum_{j \\neq i} \\rho_{ij}^2$, which decreases from $K$ (fully independent) as pairwise correlations increase. Together, $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ serve as the two predictors in the regression framework below: $\\eta_{\\max}$ indexes the weakest-link channel and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ indexes the diversity channel.\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Methods}\n\nBeyond the default majority vote, we evaluate three alternative aggregation rules to test whether the diversity-vulnerability association persists under different decision mechanisms. The aggregation comparison uses the same log-log regression specification as the primary analysis (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}).\n\n\\paragraph{Majority Vote.} Accept if $\\sum_{j=1}^K \\mathbf{1}[J_j \\text{ approves}] \\geq \\lceil K/2 \\rceil$ (baseline).\n\n\\paragraph{Accuracy-Weighted Vote.} Weight judges by clean-data accuracy: $w_j \\propto \\mathrm{acc}_j$, $\\sum_j w_j = 1$; accept if $\\sum_j w_j \\cdot \\mathbf{1}[J_j \\text{ approves}] > 0.5$.\n\n\\paragraph{Threshold Vote (Unanimity).} Accept only if all $K$ judges approve ($t = K$).\n\n\\paragraph{Bayesian Calibration.} Compute $P(\\text{correct} \\mid \\mathbf{v}) \\propto P(\\text{correct}) \\prod_j P(v_j \\mid \\text{correct})$ using each judge's confusion matrix on clean data; accept if the posterior exceeds 0.5.\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Framework}\n\\label{sec:stat_framework}\n\nTo disentangle the contributions of panel diversity and individual judge robustness, we fit a log-log OLS regression for each of the 12 conditions:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:regression}\n\\log(\\mathrm{ASR} + \\epsilon) = \\alpha + \\beta_1 \\log(K_{\\mathrm{eff}}) + \\beta_2 \\log(\\eta_{\\max} + \\epsilon),\n\\end{equation}\nwith $\\epsilon = 10^{-6}$ to handle zero values (sensitivity to $\\epsilon$ is analyzed in Appendix~\\ref{app:epsilon}). Standardized coefficients $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}} = \\hat{\\beta} \\cdot \\mathrm{SD}(\\log x) / \\mathrm{SD}(\\log y)$ enable direct comparison of effect magnitudes across predictors and conditions. We apply Benjamini-Hochberg correction for multiple testing, controlling the false discovery rate per predictor across the 12 conditions.\n\nTo assess relative predictor importance beyond regression coefficients, we perform dominance analysis \\citep{budescu1993dominance}. This method decomposes $R^2$ by averaging each predictor's incremental $R^2$ contribution across all possible subsets of predictors. A predictor is \\emph{dominant} if its average contribution exceeds that of the competing predictor in every subset ordering. With two predictors, dominance reduces to comparing each predictor's solo $R^2$ with its incremental $R^2$ conditional on the other.\n\nTo test whether $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s effect direction varies by attack type, we fit generalized additive models (GAMs) with smooth terms for both predictors, allowing nonlinear partial effects. The GAM partial effect of $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is then examined separately for targeted attacks (prompt injection, score manipulation, position bias) and shared-bias attacks (sycophancy, verbosity bias, authority bias).\n\n\\subsection{Robustness to Non-Independence}\n\\label{sec:robustness_method}\n\nThe 455 panels share judges combinatorially (each appears in 91 panels), inducing correlated residuals. We correct for this using clustered standard errors (CR1, 3-position max-SE), mixed-effects models (random judge intercepts, 3-position max-$p$), and wild cluster bootstrap ($B{=}9{,}999$, Rademacher weights, 3-position max-$p$) following \\citet{cameron2008bootstrap}. The 13 clusters per position are below the conventional 20-cluster threshold, making the max-$p$ aggregation important for conservative inference. We use the log-log specification rather than fractional response models \\citep{papke1996econometric} or beta regression \\citep{ferrari2004beta} because the log transform linearizes the multiplicative SDPI structure. Details are in Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_methods}. As a sensitivity check, we exclude judges with clean accuracy below 0.5 and verify that $\\eta_{\\max}$ effects remain stable.\n",

The 13 clusters per position are below the conventional 20-cluster threshold, making the max-$p$ aggregation important for conservative inference. 85	\label{tab:cross_k}

87	
88	Table~\ref{tab:cross_k} reveals a sharp regime transition between $K{=}3$ and $K{=}5$. At $K{=}3$, $\eta_{\max}$ is the dominant predictor (5/12 significant) and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is negligible (1/12). At $K{=}5$, this pattern reverses: $\eta_{\max}$ drops to 0/12 while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ rises to 7/12, all positive. The transition persists at $K{=}7$ (0/12, 5/12). The $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient does not shift from positive to negative across $K$; it is positive in every significant condition at every panel size. Instead, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ transitions from a negligible to a significant positive predictor of panel vulnerability at the aggregate level.
89	
90	All significant $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficients are positive across all $K$ values. The sole negative outlier (ARC $\times$ sycophancy) does not reach FDR significance at any $K \geq 5$. The attenuation from 7/12 at $K{=}5$ to 5/12 at $K{=}7$ reflects increased conservatism of the 7-position max-$p$ correction (\S\ref{sec:analysis}).
91	
92	\subsection{Random vs.\ Adaptive Fault Contrast}
93	\label{sec:rf_contrast}
94	
95	We simulate non-adversarial noise by randomly flipping judge votes at observed per-judge error rates. At $K{=}3$, random faults yield $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$ with $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ bootstrap-significant in 2/2 conditions ($\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}} = +0.215$ and $+0.379$); under adaptive attack, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s significance collapses (1/12). At $K{=}5$, random faults yield $R^2 = 0.705$ ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 2/2 significant), while adaptive attack reduces $R^2$ to 0.256, but $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ retains significant predictive power (7/12). The random-to-adaptive gap narrows from ${\sim}0.6$ ($K{=}3$) to $0.45$ ($K{=}5$), confirming that the contrast is most pronounced in small panels where $\eta_{\max}$ dominates.
96	
97	\begin{figure}[t]
98	\centering
99	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_gam_keff_split.pdf}
100	\caption{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ effect direction split by attack type at $K{=}3$ (GAM with FDR correction). Targeted attacks show positive $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (higher diversity associated with higher vulnerability), while shared-bias attacks show negative $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (higher diversity associated with lower vulnerability). At $K{=}5$, 11/12 conditions retain this pattern.}
101	\label{fig:gam_split}
102	\end{figure}
103	
104	\subsection{Attack-Type Taxonomy}
105	\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}
106	
107	GAM analysis reveals structure masked by the significance collapse (Figure~\ref{fig:gam_split}). At $K{=}3$, the 12 conditions split 6:6 by attack mechanism. \textit{Targeted attacks} (prompt injection, score manipulation, position bias) show positive $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ in all 6 conditions, consistent with diverse panels exposing more distinct weaknesses. \textit{Shared-bias attacks} (sycophancy, verbosity bias, authority bias) show negative $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ in all 6, consistent with diverse panels diluting shared susceptibility. At $K{=}5$, the split is largely preserved (7:5): one shared-bias condition (MMLU $\times$ sycophancy) flips positive, but the remaining 11/12 conditions maintain the targeted/shared-bias distinction.
108	
109	\subsection{Aggregation Rules Modulate the Diversity Channel}
110	\label{sec:aggregation}
111	
112	Table~\ref{tab:aggregation} reports regression results for four aggregation methods across all 12 conditions.
113

119	\textbf{Method} & \textbf{$\eta_{\max}$ sig} & \textbf{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} & \textbf{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ $\beta$ dir.} & \textbf{$R^2$} \\

122	Majority        & 9/12  & 8/12  & 7+/1$-$   & 0.298 \\

127	\caption{Aggregation method comparison ($K{=}3$, 15 models, 455 panels, log-log canonical spec). Significance counts are FDR-corrected across 12 conditions. $R^2$ is the mean across conditions. Methods are ordered by $R^2$ to highlight the vulnerability gradient from threshold (most predictable) to Bayesian (least).}
128	\label{tab:aggregation}
129	\end{table}
130	
131	
132	The methods form a vulnerability gradient from threshold ($R^2{=}0.89$) to Bayesian ($R^2{=}0.104$; Figure in Appendix~\ref{app:aggregation_details}). Threshold voting's high $R^2$ is partly structural: unanimity ($t{=}K$) makes panel failure nearly deterministic given the weakest judge, inflating predictability. Bayesian calibration reverses the diversity channel: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ direction flips from predominantly positive (7+/1$-$) to predominantly negative (1+/6$-$).

At $K{=}3$, random faults yield $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$ with $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ bootstrap-significant in 2/2 conditions ($\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}} = +0.215$ and $+0.379$); under adaptive attack, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s significance collapses (1/12). At $K{=}5$, random faults yield $R^2 = 0.705$ ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 2/2 significant), while adaptive attack reduces $R^2$ to 0.256, but $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ retains significant predictive power (7/12). The random-to-adaptive gap narrows from ${\\sim}0.6$ ($K{=}3$) to $0.45$ ($K{=}5$), confirming that the contrast is most pronounced in small panels where $\\eta_{\\max}$ dominates.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_gam_keff_split.pdf}\n\\caption{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ effect direction split by attack type at $K{=}3$ (GAM with FDR correction). Targeted attacks show positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (higher diversity associated with higher vulnerability), while shared-bias attacks show negative $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (higher diversity associated with lower vulnerability). At $K{=}5$, 11/12 conditions retain this pattern.}\n\\label{fig:gam_split}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Attack-Type Taxonomy}\n\\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}\n\nGAM analysis reveals structure masked by the significance collapse (Figure~\\ref{fig:gam_split}). At $K{=}3$, the 12 conditions split 6:6 by attack mechanism. \\textit{Targeted attacks} (prompt injection, score manipulation, position bias) show positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ in all 6 conditions, consistent with diverse panels exposing more distinct weaknesses. \\textit{Shared-bias attacks} (sycophancy, verbosity bias, authority bias) show negative $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ in all 6, consistent with diverse panels diluting shared susceptibility. At $K{=}5$, the split is largely preserved (7:5): one shared-bias condition (MMLU $\\times$ sycophancy) flips positive, but the remaining 11/12 conditions maintain the targeted/shared-bias distinction.\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Rules Modulate the Diversity Channel}\n\\label{sec:aggregation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:aggregation} reports regression results for four aggregation methods across all 12 conditions.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{$\\eta_{\\max}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ $\\beta$ dir.} & \\textbf{$R^2$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nThreshold       & 12/12 & 11/12 & 10+/1$-$  & 0.89 \\\\\nMajority        & 9/12  & 8/12  & 7+/1$-$   & 0.298 \\\\\nWeighted        & 9/12  & 9/12  & 9+/0$-$   & 0.284 \\\\\nBayesian        & 9/12  & 7/12  & 1+/6$-$   & 0.104 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Aggregation method comparison ($K{=}3$, 15 models, 455 panels, log-log canonical spec). Significance counts are FDR-corrected across 12 conditions. $R^2$ is the mean across conditions. Methods are ordered by $R^2$ to highlight the vulnerability gradient from threshold (most predictable) to Bayesian (least).}\n\\label{tab:aggregation}\n\\end{table}\n\n\nThe methods form a vulnerability gradient from threshold ($R^2{=}0.89$) to Bayesian ($R^2{=}0.104$; Figure in Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_details}). Threshold voting's high $R^2$ is partly structural: unanimity ($t{=}K$) makes panel failure nearly deterministic given the weakest judge, inflating predictability. Bayesian calibration reverses the diversity channel: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ direction flips from predominantly positive (7+/1$-$) to predominantly negative (1+/6$-$).\n",
      "numLines": 49,
      "startLine": 85,
      "totalLines": 133
    }

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_011WL9MtJ13KuqkFgAP93fBr`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Analysis}
2	\label{sec:analysis}
3	
4	\paragraph{Regime Transition Mechanism.}
5	At $K{=}3$, a majority requires $t{=}2$; a single compromised judge flips the panel, making $\eta_{\max}$ the binding constraint. At $K{\geq}5$, the adversary must compromise ${\geq}3$ judges; high-$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ panels may present more distinct attack surfaces, consistent with the observed positive coefficient, while low-$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ panels share robust consensus. The transition threshold $K^*$ lies between 3 and 5.
6	
7	\paragraph{Attack-Mechanism-Dependent Structure.}
8	The aggregate positive $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient masks a 6:6 directional split at $K{=}3$ (11/12 consistent at $K{=}5$; Appendix~\ref{app:bft_reversal}). Targeted attacks are consistent with judge-specific vulnerabilities amplified by independence; shared-bias attacks are consistent with common biases diluted by diversity. Unconditional claims about diversity being ``harmful'' or ``beneficial'' are thus misleading.
9	
10	\paragraph{Diversity--Capability Confound.}
11	The $r{=}0.70$ correlation between $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ ($r{\approx}0.82$ in log space) reflects a structural property: maximizing diversity requires sampling across capability tiers, introducing weaker judges. VIF averages 3.0 (10/12 below 5). After controlling for $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$, $\eta_{\max}$ retains partial $r{=}0.31$ (unique variance ${\approx}9.6\%$); $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s partial $r{=}0.12$ (${\approx}1.4\%$). This asymmetry shows the confound does not fully explain $\eta_{\max}$'s advantage, but observational regression cannot reliably disentangle diversity's causal contribution from the co-occurring capability deficit (Appendix~\ref{app:diversity_capability}). Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity}) reveals heterogeneous robustness: RV ranges from 0.005 to 0.52 across 36 conditions, with the known $\eta_{\max}$ confound exceeding this threshold in 28/36 conditions. Robustness increases with $K$ (RV exceeds $\eta_{\max}$ in 1/12 at $K{=}3$, 3/12 at $K{=}5$, 4/12 at $K{=}7$), paralleling the regime transition; this motivates the capability-matched replication below. The transition does not replicate in the API-only subset ($K{=}5$: $r(K_{\mathrm{eff}}, \eta_{\max}){=}0.85$, VIF${=}4.4$), where collinearity partially inflates $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ standard errors (${\sim}2{\times}$), attenuating its significance from 7/12 (full pool) to 3/12. However, capability-matched subsamples that break this collinearity ($r{=}0.50$) strengthen $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s emergence to 9/12, exceeding the full-pool 7/12 (Appendix~\ref{app:robustness_checks}). This pool dependence is itself informative: the transition requires sufficient capability diversity to manifest, rather than being an artifact of including weak judges. Cross-benchmark prediction further supports this interpretation: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ computed from MMLU MI predicts ARC vulnerability in 14/18 bootstrap conditions, with cross-benchmark $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ correlations of $r{\approx}0.76$--$0.78$ (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_benchmark}).
12	
13	\paragraph{Aggregation and Deployment.}
14	At $K{=}3$, Bayesian calibration reverses $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction (6/7 significant coefficients negative) by downweighting weak judges via calibrated confusion matrices (Table~\ref{tab:aggregation}); at $K{=}5$, Bayesian aggregation neutralizes both predictors ($\eta_{\max}$ 1/12, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 0/12; Table~\ref{tab:aggregation_k5}), eliminating panel composition's predictive power entirely. Robustness-first selection ($\min \eta_{\max}$) achieves lowest ASR in 12/12 conditions at $K{=}3$ (Appendix~\ref{app:panel_selection}). Practitioners should match strategy to panel size: screen for individual robustness at $K{=}3$; prefer correlated robustness profiles at $K{\geq}5$. $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is computed from clean pairwise MI; recomputing MI under attacked conditions confirms stability (Spearman $\rho{\geq}0.91$ for 4/6 attacks), though position bias substantially disrupts MI structure ($\rho{=}0.08$ on ARC), consistent with its anomalous regression behavior (Appendix~\ref{app:attacked_keff}). Fractional logit regression \citep{papke1996econometric} yields 93.1\% directional agreement with OLS and preserves the transition pattern (Appendix~\ref{app:robustness_checks}). The random-fault contrast (\S\ref{sec:rf_contrast}) shows $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ predicts under non-adaptive noise ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$), with the predictability collapse under adaptive attack most pronounced at $K{=}3$ (1/12) and attenuated at $K{\geq}5$ (7/12).
15	
```

However, capability-matched subsamples that break this collinearity ($r{=}0.50$) strengthen $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s emergence to 9/12, exceeding the full-pool 7/12 (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}). This pool dependence is itself informative: the transition requires sufficient capability diversity to manifest, rather than being an artifact of including weak judges. Cross-benchmark prediction further supports this interpretation: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ computed from MMLU MI predicts ARC vulnerability in 14/18 bootstrap conditions, with cross-benchmark $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ correlations of $r{\\approx}0.76$--$0.78$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_benchmark}).\n\n\\paragraph{Aggregation and Deployment.}\nAt $K{=}3$, Bayesian calibration reverses $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction (6/7 significant coefficients negative) by downweighting weak judges via calibrated confusion matrices (Table~\\ref{tab:aggregation}); at $K{=}5$, Bayesian aggregation neutralizes both predictors ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 1/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 0/12; Table~\\ref{tab:aggregation_k5}), eliminating panel composition's predictive power entirely. Robustness-first selection ($\\min \\eta_{\\max}$) achieves lowest ASR in 12/12 conditions at $K{=}3$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:panel_selection}). The random-fault contrast (\\S\\ref{sec:rf_contrast}) shows $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ predicts under non-adaptive noise ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$), with the predictability collapse under adaptive attack most pronounced at $K{=}3$ (1/12) and attenuated at $K{\\geq}5$ (7/12).\n",

  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Diversity--Capability Confound.}\nThe $r{=}0.70$ correlation between $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\eta_{\\max}$ reflects a structural property: maximizing diversity requires sampling across capability tiers, introducing weaker judges.
