  "uuid": "41a7710d-6252-47f7-ab09-9b8b5216a392",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/analysis.tex",
    "oldString": "\\paragraph{Diversity--Capability Confound.}\nThe $r{=}0.70$ correlation between $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\eta_{\\max}$ ($r{\\approx}0.82$ in log space) reflects a structural property: maximizing diversity requires sampling across capability tiers, introducing weaker judges. VIF averages 3.0 (10/12 below 5). After controlling for $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$, $\\eta_{\\max}$ retains partial $r{=}0.31$ (unique variance ${\\approx}9.6\\%$); $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s partial $r{=}0.12$ (${\\approx}1.4\\%$). This asymmetry shows the confound does not fully explain $\\eta_{\\max}$'s advantage, but observational regression cannot reliably disentangle diversity's causal contribution from the co-occurring capability deficit (Appendix~\\ref{app:diversity_capability}). Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity}) reveals heterogeneous robustness: RV ranges from 0.005 to 0.52 across 36 conditions, with the known $\\eta_{\\max}$ confound exceeding this threshold in 28/36 conditions. Robustness increases with $K$ (RV exceeds $\\eta_{\\max}$ in 1/12 at $K{=}3$, 3/12 at $K{=}5$, 4/12 at $K{=}7$), paralleling the regime transition; this motivates the capability-matched replication below. The transition does not replicate in the API-only subset ($K{=}5$: $r(K_{\\mathrm{eff}}, \\eta_{\\max}){=}0.85$, VIF${=}4.4$), where collinearity partially inflates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ standard errors (${\\sim}2{\\times}$), attenuating its significance from 7/12 (full pool) to 3/12. However, capability-matched subsamples that break this collinearity ($r{=}0.50$) strengthen $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s emergence to 9/12, exceeding the full-pool 7/12 (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}). This pool dependence is itself informative: the transition requires sufficient capability diversity to manifest, rather than being an artifact of including weak judges. Cross-benchmark prediction further supports this interpretation: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ computed from MMLU MI predicts ARC vulnerability in 14/18 bootstrap conditions, with cross-benchmark $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ correlations of $r{\\approx}0.76$--$0.78$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_benchmark}).",
    "newString": "\\paragraph{Diversity--Capability Confound.}\nThe $r{=}0.70$ correlation between $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\eta_{\\max}$ reflects a structural property: maximizing diversity requires sampling across capability tiers, introducing weaker judges. VIF averages 3.0 (10/12 below 5), but observational regression cannot reliably disentangle diversity's causal contribution from the co-occurring capability deficit (Appendix~\\ref{app:diversity_capability}). In the full pool, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s independent effect cannot be separated from capability variance in the majority of conditions: Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis \\citep{cinelli2020making} shows that the known $\\eta_{\\max}$ confound exceeds the robustness value in 28/36 conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity}). Robustness increases with $K$ (RV exceeds $\\eta_{\\max}$ in 1/12 at $K{=}3$, 3/12 at $K{=}5$, 4/12 at $K{=}7$), paralleling the regime transition. The API-only subset ($r{=}0.85$) does not replicate the transition (3/12), raising the possibility that $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ partly proxies for quantization-induced capability variance rather than a pure diversity signal. Capability-matched subsampling ($r{=}0.50$, 9 models, 126 panels at $K{=}5$) provides partial mitigation: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ strengthens to 9/12 and Cinelli--Hazlett RV exceeds the $\\eta_{\\max}$ confound in 6/12 conditions (conservatively 5/12 excluding near-zero $R^2$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity_poolA}), compared to 3/12 in the full pool. Cross-benchmark prediction further supports this: MMLU-derived $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ predicts ARC vulnerability in 14/18 conditions ($r{\\approx}0.76$--$0.78$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_benchmark}). The transition is thus a descriptive regime shift whose independent causal attribution to diversity requires capability-controlled experimental designs.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\n\\paragraph{Regime Transition Mechanism.}\nAt $K{=}3$, a majority requires $t{=}2$; a single compromised judge flips the panel, making $\\eta_{\\max}$ the binding constraint. At $K{\\geq}5$, the adversary must compromise ${\\geq}3$ judges, and high-$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ panels present more distinct attack surfaces. The transition threshold $K^*$ lies between 3 and 5.\n\n\\paragraph{Attack-Mechanism-Dependent Structure.}\nThe aggregate positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient masks a 6:6 directional split at $K{=}3$ (11/12 consistent at $K{=}5$; Appendix~\\ref{app:bft_reversal}). Targeted attacks are consistent with judge-specific vulnerabilities amplified by independence; shared-bias attacks are consistent with common biases diluted by diversity. Unconditional claims about diversity being ``harmful'' or ``beneficial'' are thus misleading.\n\n\\paragraph{Diversity--Capability Confound.}\nThe $r{=}0.70$ correlation between $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\eta_{\\max}$ ($r{\\approx}0.82$ in log space) reflects a structural property: maximizing diversity requires sampling across capability tiers, introducing weaker judges. VIF averages 3.0 (10/12 below 5). After controlling for $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$, $\\eta_{\\max}$ retains partial $r{=}0.31$ (unique variance ${\\approx}9.6\\%$); $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s partial $r{=}0.12$ (${\\approx}1.4\\%$). This asymmetry shows the confound does not fully explain $\\eta_{\\max}$'s advantage, but observational regression cannot reliably disentangle diversity's causal contribution from the co-occurring capability deficit (Appendix~\\ref{app:diversity_capability}). Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity}) reveals heterogeneous robustness: RV ranges from 0.005 to 0.52 across 36 conditions, with the known $\\eta_{\\max}$ confound exceeding this threshold in 28/36 conditions. Robustness increases with $K$ (RV exceeds $\\eta_{\\max}$ in 1/12 at $K{=}3$, 3/12 at $K{=}5$, 4/12 at $K{=}7$), paralleling the regime transition; this motivates the capability-matched replication below. The transition does not replicate in the API-only subset ($K{=}5$: $r(K_{\\mathrm{eff}}, \\eta_{\\max}){=}0.85$, VIF${=}4.4$), where collinearity partially inflates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ standard errors (${\\sim}2{\\times}$), attenuating its significance from 7/12 (full pool) to 3/12. However, capability-matched subsamples that break this collinearity ($r{=}0.50$) strengthen $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s emergence to 9/12, exceeding the full-pool 7/12 (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}). This pool dependence is itself informative: the transition requires sufficient capability diversity to manifest, rather than being an artifact of including weak judges. Cross-benchmark prediction further supports this interpretation: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ computed from MMLU MI predicts ARC vulnerability in 14/18 bootstrap conditions, with cross-benchmark $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ correlations of $r{\\approx}0.76$--$0.78$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_benchmark}).\n\n\\paragraph{Aggregation and Deployment.}\nAt $K{=}3$, Bayesian calibration reverses $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction (6/7 significant coefficients negative) by downweighting weak judges via calibrated confusion matrices (Table~\\ref{tab:aggregation}); at $K{=}5$, Bayesian aggregation neutralizes both predictors ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 1/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 0/12; Table~\\ref{tab:aggregation_k5}), eliminating panel composition's predictive power entirely. Robustness-first selection ($\\min \\eta_{\\max}$) achieves lowest ASR in 12/12 conditions at $K{=}3$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:panel_selection}). Practitioners should match strategy to panel size: screen for individual robustness at $K{=}3$; prefer correlated robustness profiles at $K{\\geq}5$. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is computed from clean pairwise MI; recomputing MI under attacked conditions confirms stability (Spearman $\\rho{\\geq}0.91$ for 4/6 attacks), though position bias substantially disrupts MI structure ($\\rho{=}0.08$ on ARC), consistent with its anomalous regression behavior (Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}). Fractional logit regression \\citep{papke1996econometric} yields 93.1\\% directional agreement with OLS and preserves the transition pattern (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}). The random-fault contrast (\\S\\ref{sec:rf_contrast}) shows $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ predicts under non-adaptive noise ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$), with the predictability collapse under adaptive attack most pronounced at $K{=}3$ (1/12) and attenuated at $K{\\geq}5$ (7/12).\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\n\\paragraph{Regime Transition Mechanism.}\nAt $K{=}3$, a majority requires $t{=}2$; a single compromised judge flips the panel, making $\\eta_{\\max}$ the binding constraint. At $K{\\geq}5$, the adversary must compromise ${\\geq}3$ judges, and high-$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ panels present more distinct attack surfaces. The transition threshold $K^*$ lies between 3 and 5.\n\n\\paragraph{Attack-Mechanism-Dependent Structure.}\nThe aggregate positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient masks a 6:6 directional split at $K{=}3$ (11/12 consistent at $K{=}5$; Appendix~\\ref{app:bft_reversal}). Targeted attacks are consistent with judge-specific vulnerabilities amplified by independence; shared-bias attacks are consistent with common biases diluted by diversity. Unconditional claims about diversity being ``harmful'' or ``beneficial'' are thus misleading.\n\n\\paragraph{Diversity--Capability Confound.}\nThe $r{=}0.70$ correlation between $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\eta_{\\max}$ reflects a structural property: maximizing diversity requires sampling across capability tiers, introducing weaker judges. VIF averages 3.0 (10/12 below 5), but observational regression cannot reliably disentangle diversity's causal contribution from the co-occurring capability deficit (Appendix~\\ref{app:diversity_capability}). In the full pool, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s independent effect cannot be separated from capability variance in the majority of conditions: Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis \\citep{cinelli2020making} shows that the known $\\eta_{\\max}$ confound exceeds the robustness value in 28/36 conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity}). Robustness increases with $K$ (RV exceeds $\\eta_{\\max}$ in 1/12 at $K{=}3$, 3/12 at $K{=}5$, 4/12 at $K{=}7$), paralleling the regime transition. The API-only subset ($r{=}0.85$) does not replicate the transition (3/12), raising the possibility that $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ partly proxies for quantization-induced capability variance rather than a pure diversity signal. Capability-matched subsampling ($r{=}0.50$, 9 models, 126 panels at $K{=}5$) provides partial mitigation: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ strengthens to 9/12 and Cinelli--Hazlett RV exceeds the $\\eta_{\\max}$ confound in 6/12 conditions (conservatively 5/12 excluding near-zero $R^2$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity_poolA}), compared to 3/12 in the full pool. Cross-benchmark prediction further supports this: MMLU-derived $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ predicts ARC vulnerability in 14/18 conditions ($r{\\approx}0.76$--$0.78$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_benchmark}). The transition is thus a descriptive regime shift whose independent causal attribution to diversity requires capability-controlled experimental designs.\n\n\\paragraph{Aggregation and Deployment.}\nAt $K{=}3$, Bayesian calibration reverses $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction (6/7 significant coefficients negative) by downweighting weak judges via calibrated confusion matrices (Table~\\ref{tab:aggregation}); at $K{=}5$, Bayesian aggregation neutralizes both predictors ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 1/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 0/12; Table~\\ref{tab:aggregation_k5}), eliminating panel composition's predictive power entirely. Robustness-first selection ($\\min \\eta_{\\max}$) achieves lowest ASR in 12/12 conditions at $K{=}3$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:panel_selection}). Practitioners should match strategy to panel size: screen for individual robustness at $K{=}3$; prefer correlated robustness profiles at $K{\\geq}5$. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is computed from clean pairwise MI; recomputing MI under attacked conditions confirms stability (Spearman $\\rho{\\geq}0.91$ for 4/6 attacks), though position bias substantially disrupts MI structure ($\\rho{=}0.08$ on ARC), consistent with its anomalous regression behavior (Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}). Fractional logit regression \\citep{papke1996econometric} yields 93.1\\% directional agreement with OLS and preserves the transition pattern (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}). The random-fault contrast (\\S\\ref{sec:rf_contrast}) shows $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ predicts under non-adaptive noise ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$), with the predictability collapse under adaptive attack most pronounced at $K{=}3$ (1/12) and attenuated at $K{\\geq}5$ (7/12).\n",

  "old_string": "The dominant vulnerability predictor in LLM judge panels transitions with panel size: $\\eta_{\\max}$ dominates at $K{=}3$ (bootstrap 5/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 1/12); at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominates (7/12, $\\eta_{\\max}$ 0/12). The aggregate positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ association is attack-mechanism-dependent (targeted positive, shared-bias negative); Bayesian calibration reverses it at $K{=}3$ and neutralizes both predictors at $K{=}5$. Observational regression cannot reliably disentangle diversity's causal contribution from the co-occurring capability deficit (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}). For deployment: at $K{=}3$, screen for individual robustness and use Bayesian aggregation; at $K{\\geq}5$, prefer correlated robustness profiles over maximally independent judges. Causal identification requires training-based diversity control \\citep{kariyappa2019improving,pang2019improving}; the transition threshold $K^*$ ($3 < K^* < 5$) and generalization beyond multiple-choice formats remain open.",
  "new_string": "We document a descriptive regime transition in LLM judge panel vulnerability: individual weakness ($\\eta_{\\max}$) dominates at $K{=}3$; at $K{\\geq}5$, diversity structure ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$) becomes the primary predictor. The aggregate positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ association is attack-mechanism-dependent (targeted positive, shared-bias negative); Bayesian calibration corresponds to a sign change at $K{=}3$ and renders both predictors negligible at $K{=}5$. Because $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\eta_{\\max}$ are structurally correlated ($r{=}0.70$), the independent causal contribution of diversity remains only partially separable even under capability-matched subsampling (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}). For deployment: at $K{=}3$, screen for individual robustness; at $K{\\geq}5$, prefer correlated robustness profiles. Causal identification requires training-based diversity control \\citep{kariyappa2019improving,pang2019improving}."

  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/main.tex",
  "old_string": "Our study has four principal limitations. First, the combinatorial panel design introduces non-independence that increases with $K$ ($\\binom{15}{3}{=}455$ to $\\binom{15}{7}{=}6{,}435$ panels). We address this with wild cluster bootstrap using $K$-position max-$p$, but the max-$p$ correction grows more conservative at larger $K$ (7 positions vs.\\ 3), which may partly explain the $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ attenuation from 7/12 at $K{=}5$ to 5/12 at $K{=}7$. Second, both benchmarks use multiple-choice question formats; the six local models use AWQ INT4 quantization, which reduces judge accuracy (Table~\\ref{tab:models}). Generalization to open-ended generation evaluation and full-precision deployments remains untested. Third, we study only three discrete $K$ values; the transition threshold between the $\\eta_{\\max}$-dominant and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant regimes is bounded to $3 < K^* < 5$ but not precisely identified. Fourth, the transition's strength is model-pool-dependent: it requires sufficient capability diversity ($r(K_{\\mathrm{eff}}, \\eta_{\\max}) \\lesssim 0.60$) to manifest, and is strongest when the pool spans multiple capability tiers. Training-based diversity control \\citep{kariyappa2019improving,pang2019improving} could enable causal identification in future work.",
  "new_string": "Our study has four principal limitations. First, the combinatorial panel design introduces non-independence that increases with $K$. We address this with wild cluster bootstrap using $K$-position max-$p$, but the max-$p$ correction grows more conservative at larger $K$, which may partly explain the $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ attenuation from 7/12 at $K{=}5$ to 5/12 at $K{=}7$. Second, both benchmarks use multiple-choice formats whose attack surface (answer permutation, option injection) differs structurally from open-ended evaluation (stylistic manipulation, coherence gaming); the six local models use AWQ INT4 quantization (Table~\\ref{tab:models}). Whether the regime transition holds for open-ended or full-precision settings is unknown. Third, we study only odd $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$ (majority vote threshold $t{=}\\lceil K/2\\rceil$); $K{=}4$ requires supermajority ($t{=}3$), changing the threshold mechanism. The transition threshold $K^*$ ($3 < K^* < 5$) is not precisely identified. Fourth, the transition's strength is model-pool-dependent and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s independent causal contribution remains only partially separable from co-occurring capability variance (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis})."
}

    "originalFile": "\\documentclass[11pt]{article}\n\\usepackage[review]{acl}\n\\usepackage{times}\n\\usepackage{latexsym}\n\\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}\n\\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}\n\\usepackage{microtype}\n\\usepackage{graphicx}\n\\usepackage{amsmath,amssymb,amsthm}\n\\usepackage{booktabs}\n\\usepackage{multirow}\n\\usepackage{xcolor}\n\\usepackage{subcaption}\n\n\\newtheorem{theorem}{Theorem}\n\\newtheorem{definition}{Definition}\n\\newtheorem{proposition}{Proposition}\n\n\\title{The Shifting Bottleneck: How Panel Size Modulates\\\\Adversarial Vulnerability in LLM Judge Panels}\n\n\\author{Anonymous}\n\n\\begin{document}\n\\maketitle\n\n\\begin{abstract}\nMulti-judge LLM evaluation panels are widely assumed to benefit from judge diversity, drawing on fault-tolerance intuitions from distributed systems. We empirically characterize a panel-size-dependent vulnerability regime transition by decomposing panel attack success into individual judge weakness ($\\eta_{\\max}$) and structural diversity ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$) across 15 models, six attack types, two benchmarks, and panel sizes $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ (up to 6,435 panels). At small panels, individual weakness dominates; at larger panels, diversity structure becomes the primary predictor. This aggregate pattern masks attack-mechanism-dependent structure: higher diversity is associated with increased vulnerability for targeted attacks but decreased vulnerability for shared-bias attacks. Aggregation rules further modulate the relationship, with Bayesian calibration reversing the diversity channel. The defense strategy must match the regime: small panels require screening for individual robustness; larger panels require managing judge independence.\n\\end{abstract}\n\n\\input{introduction}\n\\input{related_work}\n\\input{threat_model}\n\\input{methodology}\n\\input{results}\n\\input{analysis}\n\\input{conclusion}\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur study has four principal limitations. Second, both benchmarks use multiple-choice question formats; the six local models use AWQ INT4 quantization, which reduces judge accuracy (Table~\\ref{tab:models}). Third, we study only three discrete $K$ values; the transition threshold between the $\\eta_{\\max}$-dominant and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant regimes is bounded to $3 < K^* < 5$ but not precisely identified. Fourth, the transition's strength is model-pool-dependent: it requires sufficient capability diversity ($r(K_{\\mathrm{eff}}, \\eta_{\\max}) \\lesssim 0.60$) to manifest, and is strongest when the pool spans multiple capability tiers. Training-based diversity control \\citep{kariyappa2019improving,pang2019improving} could enable causal identification in future work.\n\n\\section*{Code and Data Availability}\nCode, the evaluation framework, and all panel-level results will be released upon acceptance. All evaluation items are sampled from publicly available benchmarks: MMLU \\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} and ARC-Challenge \\citep{clark2018arc}. The nine API-accessed judge models are served through OpenRouter; the six local judges are open-weight models (Qwen 2.5, Llama 3.1, Mistral Large) in AWQ-quantized form. Individual judge scores, panel compositions, and aggregated vulnerability metrics will be included in the release. 225	\end{figure}
226	
227	\section{Cross-Benchmark Prediction Robustness}
228	\label{app:cross_benchmark}
229	
230	To test whether the diversity--vulnerability association reflects structural panel properties rather than benchmark-specific artifacts, we use $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ computed from one benchmark's pairwise MI to predict panel vulnerability on the other. Under wild cluster bootstrap (FDR $\alpha{=}0.05$), MMLU-derived $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ predicts ARC vulnerability in 14/18 conditions across $K \in \{3, 5, 7\}$. The reverse direction (ARC$\to$MMLU) achieves 10/18, with attenuation concentrated at $K{\geq}5$ (4/12), consistent with ARC's binary format producing lower MI discriminability between judge pairs. Cross-benchmark $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ correlations are $r{\approx}0.76$--$0.78$, indicating that $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ primarily captures panel-level compositional structure rather than benchmark-specific judge interactions. The asymmetry between prediction directions further suggests that MI estimated from richer response distributions (MMLU's four-way choice) yields a more transferable diversity measure.
231	
```

  "new_string": "To test whether the diversity--vulnerability association reflects structural panel properties rather than benchmark-specific artifacts, we use $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ computed from one benchmark's pairwise MI to predict panel vulnerability on the other. Under wild cluster bootstrap (FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$), MMLU-derived $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ predicts ARC vulnerability in 14/18 conditions across $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$. The reverse direction (ARC$\\to$MMLU) achieves 10/18, with attenuation concentrated at $K{\\geq}5$ (4/12), consistent with ARC's binary format producing lower MI discriminability between judge pairs. Cross-benchmark $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ correlations are $r{\\approx}0.76$--$0.78$, indicating that $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ primarily captures panel-level compositional structure rather than benchmark-specific judge interactions. The asymmetry between prediction directions further suggests that MI estimated from richer response distributions (MMLU's four-way choice) yields a more transferable diversity measure.\n\n\\section{Pool A Cinelli--Hazlett Sensitivity}\n\\label{app:sensitivity_poolA}\n\nTo test whether reducing the $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--$\\eta_{\\max}$ collinearity strengthens $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s independent signal, we repeat the Cinelli--Hazlett analysis on the capability-matched Pool~A subsample (9 models, $r{=}0.50$, 84 panels at $K{=}3$, 126 at $K{=}5$). Table~\\ref{tab:sensitivity_poolA} compares the full-pool results with Pool~A.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Pool / $K$} & \\textbf{RV $>$ $\\eta$} & \\textbf{Median RV} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFull / $K{=}3$ & 1/12 & 0.30 & 1/12 \\\\\nFull / $K{=}5$ & 3/12 & 0.22 & 7/12 \\\\\nPool A / $K{=}3$ & 3/12 & 0.18 & --- \\\\\nPool A / $K{=}5$ & 6/12 & 0.22 & 9/12 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity comparison: full pool vs.\\ capability-matched Pool~A. RV $>$ $\\eta$: conditions where $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s robustness value exceeds the known $\\eta_{\\max}$ confound benchmark. Pool~A's reduced collinearity ($r{=}0.50$ vs.\\ $0.70$) doubles the fraction of robust conditions at both $K$ values. Conservative count at $K{=}5$: 5/12 (excluding MMLU $\\times$ authority, $R^2{\\approx}0$).}\n\\label{tab:sensitivity_poolA}\n\\end{table}\n\nAt $K{=}5$, Pool~A RV exceeds the $\\eta_{\\max}$ confound in 6/12 conditions (5/12 conservatively), concentrated in ARC (5/6 exceeds) with limited MMLU improvement (1/6). The improvement from full-pool 3/12 to Pool~A 6/12 demonstrates that reducing collinearity partially separates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s signal from capability variance, though full causal identification would require training-based diversity control.\n\n\\section{Epsilon Sensitivity Analysis}\n\\label{app:epsilon}\n\nThe log-log specification (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) uses $\\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ to handle zero ASR values. We assess sensitivity by comparing coefficients across $\\epsilon \\in \\{10^{-3}, 10^{-6}, 10^{-9}\\}$. Zero ASR rates are low on MMLU ($<$1\\% of panels across conditions) but can reach 38--40\\% on ARC for authority bias and sycophancy at $K{=}7$. Despite this variation, standardized coefficient differences between $\\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ and $\\epsilon{=}10^{-9}$ are $<$0.01, and qualitative conclusions (significance patterns, directional signs) are identical. FDR significance counts are stable across all three $\\epsilon$ values. As an $\\epsilon$-free robustness check, fractional logit regression \\citep{papke1996econometric} achieves 93.1\\% directional agreement with OLS and preserves the regime transition pattern (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}).\n\n\\section{Power Analysis Across Panel Sizes}\n\\label{app:power_analysis}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:mde} reports minimum detectable effect sizes (MDE at 80\\% power) under wild cluster bootstrap for each $K$. The sharp decrease in MDE from $K{=}3$ to $K{=}5$ (0.144 to 0.048) reflects both increased sample size ($455 \\to 3{,}003$ panels) and more clusters per position. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s non-significance at $K{=}3$ is thus an informative null (true effect $<$0.14 SD); conversely, the 7/12 significance at $K{=}5$ is not type~I error inflation, as the design can detect effects as small as 0.048 SD.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{rccc}\n\\toprule\n$\\boldsymbol{K}$ & \\textbf{Panels} & \\textbf{MDE ($\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}}$)} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} \\\\\n\\midrule\n3 & 455     & 0.144 & 1/12 \\\\\n5 & 3{,}003 & 0.048 & 7/12 \\\\\n7 & 6{,}435 & 0.036 & 5/12 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Minimum detectable effect size (80\\% power, 1{,}000 simulations, wild cluster bootstrap) by panel size. MDE decreases approximately as $1/\\sqrt{N}$, confirming that $K{=}5$/$K{=}7$ significance reflects genuine effects rather than inflated type~I error.}\n\\label{tab:mde}\n\\end{table}"
}

The reverse direction (ARC$\\to$MMLU) achieves 10/18, with attenuation concentrated at $K{\\geq}5$ (4/12), consistent with ARC's binary format producing lower MI discriminability between judge pairs. Cross-benchmark $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ correlations are $r{\\approx}0.76$--$0.78$, indicating that $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ primarily captures panel-level compositional structure rather than benchmark-specific judge interactions. The asymmetry between prediction directions further suggests that MI estimated from richer response distributions (MMLU's four-way choice) yields a more transferable diversity measure.",
          "+",
          "+\\section{Pool A Cinelli--Hazlett Sensitivity}",
          "+\\label{app:sensitivity_poolA}",
          "+",
          "+To test whether reducing the $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--$\\eta_{\\max}$ collinearity strengthens $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s independent signal, we repeat the Cinelli--Hazlett analysis on the capability-matched Pool~A subsample (9 models, $r{=}0.50$, 84 panels at $K{=}3$, 126 at $K{=}5$). Table~\\ref{tab:sensitivity_poolA} compares the full-pool results with Pool~A.",
          "+",
          "+\\begin{table}[h]",
          "+\\centering",
          "+\\small",
          "+\\begin{tabular}{lccc}",
          "+\\toprule",
          "+\\textbf{Pool / $K$} & \\textbf{RV $>$ $\\eta$} & \\textbf{Median RV} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} \\\\",
          "+\\midrule",
          "+Full / $K{=}3$ & 1/12 & 0.30 & 1/12 \\\\",
          "+Full / $K{=}5$ & 3/12 & 0.22 & 7/12 \\\\",
          "+Pool A / $K{=}3$ & 3/12 & 0.18 & --- \\\\",
          "+Pool A / $K{=}5$ & 6/12 & 0.22 & 9/12 \\\\",
          "+\\bottomrule",
          "+\\end{tabular}",
          "+\\caption{Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity comparison: full pool vs.\\ capability-matched Pool~A. RV $>$ $\\eta$: conditions where $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s robustness value exceeds the known $\\eta_{\\max}$ confound benchmark. Pool~A's reduced collinearity ($r{=}0.50$ vs.\\ $0.70$) doubles the fraction of robust conditions at both $K$ values. Conservative count at $K{=}5$: 5/12 (excluding MMLU $\\times$ authority, $R^2{\\approx}0$).}",
          "+\\label{tab:sensitivity_poolA}",
          "+\\end{table}",
          "+",
          "+At $K{=}5$, Pool~A RV exceeds the $\\eta_{\\max}$ confound in 6/12 conditions (5/12 conservatively), concentrated in ARC (5/6 exceeds) with limited MMLU improvement (1/6). The improvement from full-pool 3/12 to Pool~A 6/12 demonstrates that reducing collinearity partially separates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s signal from capability variance, though full causal identification would require training-based diversity control.",
          "+",
          "+\\section{Epsilon Sensitivity Analysis}",
          "+\\label{app:epsilon}",
          "+",
          "+The log-log specification (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) uses $\\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ to handle zero ASR values. We assess sensitivity by comparing coefficients across $\\epsilon \\in \\{10^{-3}, 10^{-6}, 10^{-9}\\}$. Zero ASR rates are low on MMLU ($<$1\\% of panels across conditions) but can reach 38--40\\% on ARC for authority bias and sycophancy at $K{=}7$. Despite this variation, standardized coefficient differences between $\\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ and $\\epsilon{=}10^{-9}$ are $<$0.01, and qualitative conclusions (significance patterns, directional signs) are identical. FDR significance counts are stable across all three $\\epsilon$ values. As an $\\epsilon$-free robustness check, fractional logit regression \\citep{papke1996econometric} achieves 93.1\\% directional agreement with OLS and preserves the regime transition pattern (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}).",
          "+",
          "+\\section{Power Analysis Across Panel Sizes}",
          "+\\label{app:power_analysis}",
          "+",
          "+Table~\\ref{tab:mde} reports minimum detectable effect sizes (MDE at 80\\% power) under wild cluster bootstrap for each $K$. The sharp decrease in MDE from $K{=}3$ to $K{=}5$ (0.144 to 0.048) reflects both increased sample size ($455 \\to 3{,}003$ panels) and more clusters per position. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s non-significance at $K{=}3$ is thus an informative null (true effect $<$0.14 SD); conversely, the 7/12 significance at $K{=}5$ is not type~I error inflation, as the design can detect effects as small as 0.048 SD.",
          "+",
          "+\\begin{table}[h]",
          "+\\centering",
          "+\\small",
          "+\\begin{tabular}{rccc}",
          "+\\toprule",
          "+$\\boldsymbol{K}$ & \\textbf{Panels} & \\textbf{MDE ($\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}}$)} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} \\\\",
          "+\\midrule",
          "+3 & 455     & 0.144 & 1/12 \\\\",
          "+5 & 3{,}003 & 0.048 & 7/12 \\\\",
          "+7 & 6{,}435 & 0.036 & 5/12 \\\\",
          "+\\bottomrule",
          "+\\end{tabular}",
          "+\\caption{Minimum detectable effect size (80\\% power, 1{,}000 simulations, wild cluster bootstrap) by panel size. MDE decreases approximately as $1/\\sqrt{N}$, confirming that $K{=}5$/$K{=}7$ significance reflects genuine effects rather than inflated type~I error.}",
          "+\\label{tab:mde}",
          "+\\end{table}"
        ]
      }
    ],

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01NWdVAHdfhEomcJu9paGNGF`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Analysis}
2	\label{sec:analysis}
3	
4	\paragraph{Regime Transition Mechanism.}
5	At $K{=}3$, a majority requires $t{=}2$; a single compromised judge flips the panel, making $\eta_{\max}$ the binding constraint. At $K{\geq}5$, the adversary must compromise ${\geq}3$ judges, and high-$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ panels present more distinct attack surfaces. The transition threshold $K^*$ lies between 3 and 5.
6	
7	\paragraph{Attack-Mechanism-Dependent Structure.}
8	The aggregate positive $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient masks a 6:6 directional split at $K{=}3$ (11/12 consistent at $K{=}5$; Appendix~\ref{app:bft_reversal}). Targeted attacks are consistent with judge-specific vulnerabilities amplified by independence; shared-bias attacks are consistent with common biases diluted by diversity. Unconditional claims about diversity being ``harmful'' or ``beneficial'' are thus misleading.
9	
10	\paragraph{Diversity--Capability Confound.}
11	The $r{=}0.70$ correlation between $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ reflects a structural property: maximizing diversity requires sampling across capability tiers, introducing weaker judges. VIF averages 3.0 (10/12 below 5), but observational regression cannot reliably disentangle diversity's causal contribution from the co-occurring capability deficit (Appendix~\ref{app:diversity_capability}). In the full pool, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s independent effect cannot be separated from capability variance in the majority of conditions: Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis \citep{cinelli2020making} shows that the known $\eta_{\max}$ confound exceeds the robustness value in 28/36 conditions (Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity}). Robustness increases with $K$ (RV exceeds $\eta_{\max}$ in 1/12 at $K{=}3$, 3/12 at $K{=}5$, 4/12 at $K{=}7$), paralleling the regime transition. The API-only subset ($r{=}0.85$) does not replicate the transition (3/12), raising the possibility that $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ partly proxies for quantization-induced capability variance rather than a pure diversity signal. Capability-matched subsampling ($r{=}0.50$, 9 models, 126 panels at $K{=}5$) provides partial mitigation: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ strengthens to 9/12 and Cinelli--Hazlett RV exceeds the $\eta_{\max}$ confound in 6/12 conditions (conservatively 5/12 excluding near-zero $R^2$; Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity_poolA}), compared to 3/12 in the full pool. Cross-benchmark prediction further supports this: MMLU-derived $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ predicts ARC vulnerability in 14/18 conditions ($r{\approx}0.76$--$0.78$; Appendix~\ref{app:cross_benchmark}). The transition is thus a descriptive regime shift whose independent causal attribution to diversity requires capability-controlled experimental designs.
12	
13	\paragraph{Aggregation and Deployment.}
14	At $K{=}3$, Bayesian calibration is associated with a reversal of $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction (6/7 significant coefficients negative) via calibrated confusion matrices (Table~\ref{tab:aggregation}); at $K{=}5$, Bayesian aggregation renders both predictors negligible ($\eta_{\max}$ 1/12, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 0/12; Table~\ref{tab:aggregation_k5}). Robustness-first selection ($\min \eta_{\max}$) achieves lowest ASR in 12/12 conditions at $K{=}3$ (Appendix~\ref{app:panel_selection}). Practitioners should match strategy to panel size: screen for individual robustness at $K{=}3$; prefer correlated robustness profiles at $K{\geq}5$. Recomputing MI under attacked conditions confirms stability ($\rho{\geq}0.91$ for 4/6 attacks), though position bias substantially disrupts MI structure ($\rho{=}0.08$ on ARC; Appendix~\ref{app:attacked_keff}). Fractional logit \citep{papke1996econometric} yields 93.1\% directional agreement with OLS (Appendix~\ref{app:robustness_checks}). The random-fault contrast (\S\ref{sec:rf_contrast}) shows $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ predicts under non-adaptive noise ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$), with the predictability collapse under adaptive attack most pronounced at $K{=}3$.
15	
```

    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\n\\paragraph{Regime Transition Mechanism.}\nAt $K{=}3$, a majority requires $t{=}2$; a single compromised judge flips the panel, making $\\eta_{\\max}$ the binding constraint. At $K{\\geq}5$, the adversary must compromise ${\\geq}3$ judges, and high-$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ panels present more distinct attack surfaces. The transition threshold $K^*$ lies between 3 and 5.\n\n\\paragraph{Attack-Mechanism-Dependent Structure.}\nThe aggregate positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient masks a 6:6 directional split at $K{=}3$ (11/12 consistent at $K{=}5$; Appendix~\\ref{app:bft_reversal}). Targeted attacks are consistent with judge-specific vulnerabilities amplified by independence; shared-bias attacks are consistent with common biases diluted by diversity. Unconditional claims about diversity being ``harmful'' or ``beneficial'' are thus misleading.\n\n\\paragraph{Diversity--Capability Confound.}\nThe $r{=}0.70$ correlation between $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\eta_{\\max}$ reflects a structural property: maximizing diversity requires sampling across capability tiers, introducing weaker judges. VIF averages 3.0 (10/12 below 5), but observational regression cannot reliably disentangle diversity's causal contribution from the co-occurring capability deficit (Appendix~\\ref{app:diversity_capability}). In the full pool, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s independent effect cannot be separated from capability variance in the majority of conditions: Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis \\citep{cinelli2020making} shows that the known $\\eta_{\\max}$ confound exceeds the robustness value in 28/36 conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity}). Robustness increases with $K$ (RV exceeds $\\eta_{\\max}$ in 1/12 at $K{=}3$, 3/12 at $K{=}5$, 4/12 at $K{=}7$), paralleling the regime transition. The API-only subset ($r{=}0.85$) does not replicate the transition (3/12), raising the possibility that $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ partly proxies for quantization-induced capability variance rather than a pure diversity signal. Capability-matched subsampling ($r{=}0.50$, 9 models, 126 panels at $K{=}5$) provides partial mitigation: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ strengthens to 9/12 and Cinelli--Hazlett RV exceeds the $\\eta_{\\max}$ confound in 6/12 conditions (conservatively 5/12 excluding near-zero $R^2$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity_poolA}), compared to 3/12 in the full pool. Cross-benchmark prediction further supports this: MMLU-derived $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ predicts ARC vulnerability in 14/18 conditions ($r{\\approx}0.76$--$0.78$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_benchmark}). The transition is thus a descriptive regime shift whose independent causal attribution to diversity requires capability-controlled experimental designs.\n\n\\paragraph{Aggregation and Deployment.}\nAt $K{=}3$, Bayesian calibration is associated with a reversal of $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction (6/7 significant coefficients negative) via calibrated confusion matrices (Table~\\ref{tab:aggregation}); at $K{=}5$, Bayesian aggregation renders both predictors negligible ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 1/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 0/12; Table~\\ref{tab:aggregation_k5}). Robustness-first selection ($\\min \\eta_{\\max}$) achieves lowest ASR in 12/12 conditions at $K{=}3$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:panel_selection}). Fractional logit \\citep{papke1996econometric} yields 93.1\\% directional agreement with OLS (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}). The random-fault contrast (\\S\\ref{sec:rf_contrast}) shows $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ predicts under non-adaptive noise ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$), with the predictability collapse under adaptive attack most pronounced at $K{=}3$.\n",

Capability-matched subsampling ($r{=}0.50$, 9 models, 126 panels at $K{=}5$) provides partial mitigation: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ strengthens to 9/12 and Cinelli--Hazlett RV exceeds the $\\eta_{\\max}$ confound in 6/12 conditions (conservatively 5/12 excluding near-zero $R^2$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity_poolA}), compared to 3/12 in the full pool. Cross-benchmark prediction further supports this: MMLU-derived $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ predicts ARC vulnerability in 14/18 conditions ($r{\\approx}0.76$--$0.78$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_benchmark}). Cross-benchmark prediction ($r{\\approx}0.76$--$0.78$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_benchmark}) further supports structural rather than benchmark-specific origins. The transition is thus a descriptive regime shift whose causal attribution to diversity requires capability-controlled designs.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\n\\paragraph{Regime Transition Mechanism.}\nAt $K{=}3$, a majority requires $t{=}2$; a single compromised judge flips the panel, making $\\eta_{\\max}$ the binding constraint. At $K{\\geq}5$, the adversary must compromise ${\\geq}3$ judges, and high-$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ panels present more distinct attack surfaces. The transition threshold $K^*$ lies between 3 and 5.\n\n\\paragraph{Attack-Mechanism-Dependent Structure.}\nThe aggregate positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient masks a 6:6 directional split at $K{=}3$ (11/12 consistent at $K{=}5$; Appendix~\\ref{app:bft_reversal}). Targeted attacks are consistent with judge-specific vulnerabilities amplified by independence; shared-bias attacks are consistent with common biases diluted by diversity. Unconditional claims about diversity being ``harmful'' or ``beneficial'' are thus misleading.\n\n\\paragraph{Diversity--Capability Confound.}\nThe $r{=}0.70$ correlation between $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\eta_{\\max}$ reflects a structural property: maximizing diversity requires sampling across capability tiers, introducing weaker judges. VIF averages 3.0 (10/12 below 5), but observational regression cannot reliably disentangle diversity's causal contribution from the co-occurring capability deficit (Appendix~\\ref{app:diversity_capability}). In the full pool, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s independent effect cannot be separated from capability variance in the majority of conditions: Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis \\citep{cinelli2020making} shows that the known $\\eta_{\\max}$ confound exceeds the robustness value in 28/36 conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity}). Robustness increases with $K$ (RV exceeds $\\eta_{\\max}$ in 1/12 at $K{=}3$, 3/12 at $K{=}5$, 4/12 at $K{=}7$), paralleling the regime transition. The API-only subset ($r{=}0.85$) does not replicate the transition (3/12), raising the possibility that $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ partly proxies for quantization-induced capability variance rather than a pure diversity signal. Capability-matched subsampling ($r{=}0.50$, 9 models, 126 panels at $K{=}5$) provides partial mitigation: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ strengthens to 9/12 and Cinelli--Hazlett RV exceeds the $\\eta_{\\max}$ confound in 6/12 conditions (conservatively 5/12 excluding near-zero $R^2$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity_poolA}), compared to 3/12 in the full pool. Cross-benchmark prediction further supports this: MMLU-derived $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ predicts ARC vulnerability in 14/18 conditions ($r{\\approx}0.76$--$0.78$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_benchmark}). The transition is thus a descriptive regime shift whose independent causal attribution to diversity requires capability-controlled experimental designs.\n\n\\paragraph{Aggregation and Deployment.}\nBayesian calibration is associated with a reversal of $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction at $K{=}3$ (6/7 negative; Table~\\ref{tab:aggregation}) and renders both predictors negligible at $K{=}5$ (Table~\\ref{tab:aggregation_k5}). Robustness-first selection ($\\min \\eta_{\\max}$) achieves lowest ASR in 12/12 conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:panel_selection}). Recomputing MI under attack confirms stability ($\\rho{\\geq}0.91$ for 4/6 attacks; position bias disrupts MI on ARC, $\\rho{=}0.08$; Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}). The random-fault contrast (\\S\\ref{sec:rf_contrast}) confirms $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ predicts under non-adaptive noise ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$), with the predictability collapse most pronounced at $K{=}3$.\n",

55	
56	$\eta_{\max}$ remains robust: 8/12 under clustered SE, 7/12 under mixed effects, 5/12 under wild cluster bootstrap (Figure~\ref{fig:robustness}). $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ collapses under correction: OLS's 8/12 reduces to 0/12 (clustered SE) and 1/12 (bootstrap). Point estimates remain positive, but confidence intervals contain zero in 11/12 conditions (Appendix~\ref{app:forest_plot}).
57	
58	\begin{figure}[t]
59	\centering
60	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_robustness_comparison.pdf}
61	\caption{Significance counts (FDR $< 0.05$) for $\eta_{\max}$ and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ across four correction methods over 12 experimental conditions ($K{=}3$). Wild cluster bootstrap and clustered standard errors are recommended for the 15-judge combinatorial design.}
62	\label{fig:robustness}
63	\end{figure}
64	
65	Power analysis (1{,}000 iterations, 13 clusters per position) yields MDE $\beta_{\mathrm{std}} = 0.14$ at 80\% power, below the OLS estimate of 0.165. $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s non-significance at $K{=}3$ is thus an \emph{informative null}: any true effect is smaller than 0.14 SD. The sole negative coefficient (ARC $\times$ sycophancy, $\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}} = -0.192$) does not survive position-permutation bootstrap correction ($p = 0.40$). FDR counts are stable across $\epsilon \in \{10^{-3}, 10^{-6}, 10^{-9}\}$.
66	
67	\subsection{Panel-Size-Dependent Regime Transition}
68	\label{sec:cross_k}
69

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01B7TagHkmYUaW7rR6BgJGFB`</summary>

```text
73	
74	\subsection{Robustness to Non-Independence}
75	\label{sec:robustness_method}
76	
77	The 455 panels share judges combinatorially (each appears in 91 panels), inducing correlated residuals. We correct for this using clustered standard errors (CR1, 3-position max-SE), mixed-effects models (random judge intercepts, 3-position max-$p$), and wild cluster bootstrap ($B{=}9{,}999$, Rademacher weights, 3-position max-$p$) following \citet{cameron2008bootstrap}. The 13 clusters per position are below the 20-cluster threshold recommended by \citet{cameron2008bootstrap}; following \citet{mackinnon2018wild}, we use Rademacher weights, which remain conservative in few-cluster settings even when cluster sizes are unequal. We use the log-log specification rather than fractional response models \citep{papke1996econometric} or beta regression \citep{ferrari2004beta} because the log transform linearizes the multiplicative SDPI structure; fractional logit with clustered SE serves as an $\epsilon$-free robustness check (Appendix~\ref{app:robustness_checks}). Details are in Appendix~\ref{app:robustness_methods}.
78	
```

    "originalFile": "\\documentclass[11pt]{article}\n\\usepackage[review]{acl}\n\\usepackage{times}\n\\usepackage{latexsym}\n\\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}\n\\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}\n\\usepackage{microtype}\n\\usepackage{graphicx}\n\\usepackage{amsmath,amssymb,amsthm}\n\\usepackage{booktabs}\n\\usepackage{multirow}\n\\usepackage{xcolor}\n\\usepackage{subcaption}\n\n\\newtheorem{theorem}{Theorem}\n\\newtheorem{definition}{Definition}\n\\newtheorem{proposition}{Proposition}\n\n\\title{The Shifting Bottleneck: How Panel Size Modulates\\\\Adversarial Vulnerability in LLM Judge Panels}\n\n\\author{Anonymous}\n\n\\begin{document}\n\\maketitle\n\n\\begin{abstract}\nMulti-judge LLM evaluation panels are widely assumed to benefit from judge diversity, drawing on fault-tolerance intuitions from distributed systems. We empirically characterize a panel-size-dependent vulnerability regime transition by decomposing panel attack success into individual judge weakness ($\\eta_{\\max}$) and structural diversity ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$) across 15 models, six attack types, two benchmarks, and panel sizes $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ (up to 6,435 panels). At small panels, individual weakness dominates; at larger panels, diversity structure becomes the primary predictor. This aggregate pattern masks attack-mechanism-dependent structure: higher diversity is associated with increased vulnerability for targeted attacks but decreased vulnerability for shared-bias attacks. Aggregation rules further modulate the relationship, with Bayesian calibration reversing the diversity channel. The defense strategy must match the regime: small panels require screening for individual robustness; larger panels require managing judge independence.\n\\end{abstract}\n\n\\input{introduction}\n\\input{related_work}\n\\input{threat_model}\n\\input{methodology}\n\\input{results}\n\\input{analysis}\n\\input{conclusion}\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur study has four principal limitations. First, the combinatorial panel design introduces non-independence that increases with $K$. We address this with wild cluster bootstrap using $K$-position max-$p$, but the max-$p$ correction grows more conservative at larger $K$, which may partly explain the $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ attenuation from 7/12 at $K{=}5$ to 5/12 at $K{=}7$. Second, both benchmarks use multiple-choice formats whose attack surface (answer permutation, option injection) differs structurally from open-ended evaluation (stylistic manipulation, coherence gaming); the six local models use AWQ INT4 quantization (Table~\\ref{tab:models}). Whether the regime transition holds for open-ended or full-precision settings is unknown. Third, we study only odd $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$ (majority vote threshold $t{=}\\lceil K/2\\rceil$); $K{=}4$ requires supermajority ($t{=}3$), changing the threshold mechanism. The transition threshold $K^*$ ($3 < K^* < 5$) is not precisely identified. Fourth, the transition's strength is model-pool-dependent and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s independent causal contribution remains only partially separable from co-occurring capability variance (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\n\\section*{Code and Data Availability}\nCode, the evaluation framework, and all panel-level results will be released upon acceptance. All evaluation items are sampled from publicly available benchmarks: MMLU \\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} and ARC-Challenge \\citep{clark2018arc}. The nine API-accessed judge models are served through OpenRouter; the six local judges are open-weight models (Qwen 2.5, Llama 3.1, Mistral Large) in AWQ-quantized form. Individual judge scores, panel compositions, and aggregated vulnerability metrics will be included in the release. All experiments use a fixed random seed (42), and all hyperparameters are reported in the methodology section to support reproducibility.\n\n\\input{appendix}\n\\input{appendix_attacks}\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\end{document}\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig_framework.pdf}\n\\caption{Overview of our evaluation framework. Given $N$ candidate LLM judges, we construct $K$-judge panels (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}), subject them to six adversarial attack types (\\S\\ref{sec:threat_model}), and assess vulnerability through two complementary metrics: $\\eta_{\\max}$ (weakest-link individual susceptibility) and $K_{\\text{eff}}$ (effective independent channels via mutual information). Panel decisions are aggregated through four methods of increasing sophistication (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}).}\n\\label{fig:framework}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nMulti-judge panels have emerged as the dominant defense against bias and manipulation in LLM-based evaluation. After \\citet{zheng2023judging} demonstrated that individual LLM judges can rival human annotators, subsequent work exposed systematic vulnerabilities: position bias, verbosity preferences, and sycophancy compromise the reliability of any single judge \\citep{wang2023fair}. The natural response has been to aggregate decisions across diverse models, drawing inspiration from Byzantine fault tolerance (BFT), where majority voting among independent nodes tolerates faulty responses \\citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}. A recent systematization of LLM-judge security reports that 7-model committees reduce attack success rates from 50--68\\% to 10--19\\% \\citep{almasoud2026security}. Diversity, by this logic, is the key ingredient for secure evaluation.\n\nYet the evidence for diversity as a causal defense is weaker than it appears. The observed ASR reduction conflates panel diversity (judge independence) with individual robustness (strongest members). No existing work disentangles these across panel sizes. BFT adversaries control faulty nodes internally, whereas evaluation adversaries manipulate inputs externally; the SDPI bounds joint failure but assumes non-adaptive faults \\citep{schneier2000secrets}.\n\nIn our experiments, we observe a panel-size-dependent vulnerability regime transition. At $K{=}3$, $\\eta_{\\max}$ dominates; at $K{\\geq}5$, this reverses ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominant, $\\eta_{\\max}$ non-significant). At the aggregate level, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is positively associated with vulnerability in both regimes, consistent with one possible mechanism where independent judges present more targetable attack surfaces \\citep{goodfellow2015adversarial,papernot2016transferability}. However, this pattern is attack-mechanism-dependent: GAM analysis shows higher $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ associated with \\emph{increased} vulnerability for targeted attacks but \\emph{decreased} vulnerability for shared-bias attacks, and Bayesian calibration reverses the direction entirely.\n\nControlled experiments across 15 models, $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$, 6 attacks, and 2 benchmarks confirm this transition (Figure~\\ref{fig:framework}). Under random faults, panel composition is highly predictive ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$); the predictability collapse under adaptive attack is most pronounced at $K{=}3$. Bayesian calibration reverses $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction at $K{=}3$ and neutralizes both predictors at $K{=}5$; robustness-first selection dominates diversity-maximizing strategies.\n\nThese findings yield three contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{K-dependent regime transition.} While CJT predicts that panel size affects aggregation quality, we quantify a specific transition: $\\eta_{\\max}$ dominates at $K{=}3$ (bootstrap 5/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 1/12, MDE${=}0.14$); at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the primary predictor (7/12, $\\eta_{\\max}$ 0/12). Beyond this aggregate pattern, we document attack-mechanism-dependent structure (6:6 targeted/shared-bias split) and aggregation modulation (Bayesian reversal), neither predicted by CJT (\\S\\ref{sec:dominance}, \\S\\ref{sec:cross_k}, \\S\\ref{sec:rf_contrast}).\n\\item \\textbf{Attack-type taxonomy.} GAM reveals a 6:6 split by attack mechanism at $K{=}3$ (targeted vs.\\ shared-bias), 11/12 consistent at $K{=}5$. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient positive at aggregate level but direction depends on attack mechanism (\\S\\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).\n\\item \\textbf{Practical defense recommendations.} Robustness-first selection ($\\min \\eta_{\\max}$) dominates diversity-maximizing strategies. Bayesian calibration reverses $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction at $K{=}3$ and neutralizes both predictors at $K{=}5$ (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

    "newString": "Bayesian calibration corresponds to a sign change at $K{=}3$ and renders both predictors negligible at $K{=}5$ (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig_framework.pdf}\n\\caption{Overview of our evaluation framework. Given $N$ candidate LLM judges, we construct $K$-judge panels (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}), subject them to six adversarial attack types (\\S\\ref{sec:threat_model}), and assess vulnerability through two complementary metrics: $\\eta_{\\max}$ (weakest-link individual susceptibility) and $K_{\\text{eff}}$ (effective independent channels via mutual information). Panel decisions are aggregated through four methods of increasing sophistication (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}).}\n\\label{fig:framework}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nMulti-judge panels have emerged as the dominant defense against bias and manipulation in LLM-based evaluation. After \\citet{zheng2023judging} demonstrated that individual LLM judges can rival human annotators, subsequent work exposed systematic vulnerabilities: position bias, verbosity preferences, and sycophancy compromise the reliability of any single judge \\citep{wang2023fair}. The natural response has been to aggregate decisions across diverse models, drawing inspiration from Byzantine fault tolerance (BFT), where majority voting among independent nodes tolerates faulty responses \\citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}. A recent systematization of LLM-judge security reports that 7-model committees reduce attack success rates from 50--68\\% to 10--19\\% \\citep{almasoud2026security}. Diversity, by this logic, is the key ingredient for secure evaluation.\n\nYet the evidence for diversity as a causal defense is weaker than it appears. The observed ASR reduction conflates panel diversity (judge independence) with individual robustness (strongest members). No existing work disentangles these across panel sizes. BFT adversaries control faulty nodes internally, whereas evaluation adversaries manipulate inputs externally; the SDPI bounds joint failure but assumes non-adaptive faults \\citep{schneier2000secrets}.\n\nIn our experiments, we observe a panel-size-dependent vulnerability regime transition. At $K{=}3$, $\\eta_{\\max}$ dominates; at $K{\\geq}5$, this reverses ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominant, $\\eta_{\\max}$ non-significant). At the aggregate level, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is positively associated with vulnerability in both regimes, consistent with one possible mechanism where independent judges present more targetable attack surfaces \\citep{goodfellow2015adversarial,papernot2016transferability}. However, this pattern is attack-mechanism-dependent: GAM analysis shows higher $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ associated with \\emph{increased} vulnerability for targeted attacks but \\emph{decreased} vulnerability for shared-bias attacks, and Bayesian calibration reverses the direction entirely.\n\nControlled experiments across 15 models, $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$, 6 attacks, and 2 benchmarks confirm this transition (Figure~\\ref{fig:framework}). Under random faults, panel composition is highly predictive ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$); the predictability collapse under adaptive attack is most pronounced at $K{=}3$. Bayesian calibration corresponds to a sign change in $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction at $K{=}3$ and renders both predictors negligible at $K{=}5$; robustness-first selection dominates diversity-maximizing strategies.\n\nThese findings yield three contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{K-dependent regime transition.} While CJT predicts that panel size affects aggregation quality, we quantify a specific transition: $\\eta_{\\max}$ dominates at $K{=}3$ (bootstrap 5/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 1/12, MDE${=}0.14$); at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the primary predictor (7/12, $\\eta_{\\max}$ 0/12). Beyond this aggregate pattern, we document attack-mechanism-dependent structure (6:6 targeted/shared-bias split) and aggregation modulation (Bayesian reversal), neither predicted by CJT (\\S\\ref{sec:dominance}, \\S\\ref{sec:cross_k}, \\S\\ref{sec:rf_contrast}).\n\\item \\textbf{Attack-type taxonomy.} GAM reveals a 6:6 split by attack mechanism at $K{=}3$ (targeted vs.\\ shared-bias), 11/12 consistent at $K{=}5$. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient positive at aggregate level but direction depends on attack mechanism (\\S\\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).\n\\item \\textbf{Practical defense recommendations.} Robustness-first selection ($\\min \\eta_{\\max}$) dominates diversity-maximizing strategies. Bayesian calibration reverses $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction at $K{=}3$ and neutralizes both predictors at $K{=}5$ (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {

        "oldLines": 5,
        "newStart": 19,
        "newLines": 5,

Beyond this aggregate pattern, we document attack-mechanism-dependent structure (6:6 targeted/shared-bias split) and aggregation modulation (Bayesian reversal), neither predicted by CJT (\\S\\ref{sec:dominance}, \\S\\ref{sec:cross_k}, \\S\\ref{sec:rf_contrast}).",

```text
13	
14	In our experiments, we observe a panel-size-dependent vulnerability regime transition. At $K{=}3$, $\eta_{\max}$ dominates; at $K{\geq}5$, this reverses ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ dominant, $\eta_{\max}$ non-significant). At the aggregate level, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is positively associated with vulnerability in both regimes, consistent with one possible mechanism where independent judges present more targetable attack surfaces \citep{goodfellow2015adversarial,papernot2016transferability}. However, this pattern is attack-mechanism-dependent: GAM analysis shows higher $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ associated with \emph{increased} vulnerability for targeted attacks but \emph{decreased} vulnerability for shared-bias attacks, and Bayesian calibration corresponds to a sign change.
15	
```

12	\paragraph{Adversarial Robustness and Transferability.}
13	Adversarial vulnerability was formalized by \citet{goodfellow2015adversarial}, with transferability demonstrated by \citet{papernot2016transferability,papernot2017practical}. \citet{tramer2018ensemble} introduced ensemble adversarial training but did not eliminate cross-model transfer. Controlled diversity via uncorrelated gradients \citep{kariyappa2019improving} or diversity regularization \citep{pang2019improving} strengthens ensemble robustness in vision, but requires coordinating member training, a control absent in post-hoc LLM judge selection.
```

</details>

_source line 4863 · 2026-05-23T13:09:18.483Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01MqpkL39WtdANu3qhiytx6u`</summary>

```text
41	\label{app:aggregation_details}
42	
43	Bayesian calibration flips the $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient from predominantly positive under majority vote to predominantly negative (6 of 7 significant coefficients negative). The mechanism is calibrated reliability weighting: by estimating each judge's confusion matrix on clean data, Bayesian aggregation downweights weak judges, effectively implementing robustness screening within the aggregation rule itself. Threshold voting amplifies both channels because any single dissenting judge vetoes the panel decision, making compositional factors almost perfectly predictive of attack success ($R^2{=}0.89$). Bayesian calibration requires a clean accuracy prior, and an adaptive attacker aware of this calibration could target the prior itself \citep{tambe2011security}.
```

</details>

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   The Director is coordinating Paper Agent to revise an EMNLP 2026 (ARR) paper "The Shifting Bottleneck: How Panel Size Modulates Adversarial Vulnerability in LLM Judge Panels." This session covers:
   - **Confirmation of previous Attacked K_eff table patch** (all 12 ρ values verified correct)
   - **R3 審稿響應 — 17 條 issue 整批修復**: A massive batch fix addressing 3 CRITICAL, 11 WARNING, and 4 INFO reviewer issues, with strict 8-page main text constraint
   - **URGENT correction**: Verbosity MMLU ρ was incorrectly changed from 0.91 to 0.92 — must be reverted to 0.91

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - K-dependent vulnerability regime transition: K=3 η_max dominates (5/12), K≥5 K_eff dominates (7/12)
   - Cinelli-Hazlett omitted variable bias: RV ranges 0.005–0.522, η_max exceeds RV in 28/36 conditions
   - Pool A capability-matched subsample: 9 models, r=0.50, RV exceeds η_max in 6/12 (conservative 5/12) at K=5 vs 3/12 in full pool
   - K_eff-stable attacks: 4 attacks with ρ≥0.91 yield 4/8 significant at K=5
   - Power analysis MDE: K=3: 0.144, K=5: 0.048, K=7: 0.036
   - Stochastic dominance tier breakdown: within-tier 67.7% non-dominant, cross-tier 31.1%
   - Epsilon sensitivity: coefficients differ <0.01 between ε=10⁻⁶ and 10⁻⁹
   - Causal language corrections: "reverses"→"is associated with a sign change", "eliminating"→"rendering negligible"
   - CJT novelty framing: attack-type-dependent structure (6:6 split) and aggregation-diversity interaction go beyond CJT predictions
   - Bib correction: arXiv:2402.14016 is by Raina, Liusie, Gales (NOT Shi et al.)
   - EMNLP 2026: 8-page main text limit, unlimited references/appendix
   - Paper Agent communicates via `paper_agent_post_to_director` (NOT `main_agent_post_to_director`)

3. Files and Code Sections:

   - **`references.bib`** — Bibliography database
     - Fixed shi2024judgerobust → raina2024judgerobust (correct authors: Vyas Raina, Adian Liusie, Mark Gales; correct title: "Is LLM-as-a-Judge Robust?")
     - Added 5 new entries: mackinnon2018wild, austensmith1996information, stureborg2024inconsistent, ye2024justice, cinelli2020making
     - All verified via WebSearch

   - **`main.tex`** — Abstract + Limitations + document structure
     - Abstract rewritten: "We discover" → "We empirically characterize"; removed specific sig fractions; simplified to qualitative descriptions; "reversing" → "corresponding to a sign change"
     - Current abstract (key text): `We empirically characterize a panel-size-dependent vulnerability regime transition by decomposing panel attack success into individual judge weakness ($\eta_{\max}$) and structural diversity ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$)...At small panels, individual weakness dominates; at larger panels, diversity structure becomes the primary predictor...with Bayesian calibration corresponding to a sign change in the diversity channel.`
     - Limitations rewritten: Added K=4/odd-K ("K=4 requires supermajority (t=3), changing the threshold mechanism"); Added MCQ attack surface ("answer permutation, option injection differs structurally from open-ended evaluation"); Compressed 4th limitation to reference causal separability
     - Current limitations text: `Our study has four principal limitations. First, the combinatorial panel design introduces non-independence...Second, both benchmarks use multiple-choice formats whose attack surface (answer permutation, option injection) differs structurally from open-ended evaluation (stylistic manipulation, coherence gaming)...Third, we study only odd $K \in \{3,5,7\}$ (majority vote threshold $t{=}\lceil K/2\rceil$); $K{=}4$ requires supermajority ($t{=}3$)...Fourth, the transition's strength is model-pool-dependent and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}s independent causal contribution remains only partially separable from co-occurring capability variance`

   - **`abstract.tex`** — Mirror of main.tex abstract, synced

   - **`introduction.tex`** — Introduction section

        "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig_framework.pdf}\n\\caption{Overview of our evaluation framework. Given $N$ candidate LLM judges, we construct $K$-judge panels (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}), subject them to six adversarial attack types (\\S\\ref{sec:threat_model}), and assess vulnerability through two complementary metrics: $\\eta_{\\max}$ (weakest-link individual susceptibility) and $K_{\\text{eff}}$ (effective independent channels via mutual information). Panel decisions are aggregated through four methods of increasing sophistication (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}).}\n\\label{fig:framework}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nMulti-judge panels have emerged as the dominant defense against bias and manipulation in LLM-based evaluation. After \\citet{zheng2023judging} demonstrated that individual LLM judges can rival human annotators, subsequent work exposed systematic vulnerabilities: position bias, verbosity preferences, and sycophancy compromise the reliability of any single judge \\citep{wang2023fair}. The natural response has been to aggregate decisions across diverse models, drawing inspiration from Byzantine fault tolerance (BFT), where majority voting among independent nodes tolerates faulty responses \\citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}. A recent systematization of LLM-judge security reports that 7-model committees reduce attack success rates from 50--68\\% to 10--19\\% \\citep{almasoud2026security}. Diversity, by this logic, is the key ingredient for secure evaluation.\n\nYet the evidence for diversity as a causal defense is weaker than it appears. The observed ASR reduction conflates panel diversity (judge independence) with individual robustness (strongest members). No existing work disentangles these across panel sizes. BFT adversaries control faulty nodes internally, whereas evaluation adversaries manipulate inputs externally; the SDPI bounds joint failure but assumes non-adaptive faults \\citep{schneier2000secrets}.\n\nIn our experiments, we observe a panel-size-dependent vulnerability regime transition. At $K{=}3$, $\\eta_{\\max}$ dominates; at $K{\\geq}5$, this reverses ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominant, $\\eta_{\\max}$ non-significant). At the aggregate level, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is positively associated with vulnerability in both regimes, consistent with one possible mechanism where independent judges present more targetable attack surfaces \\citep{goodfellow2015adversarial,papernot2016transferability}. However, this pattern is attack-mechanism-dependent: GAM analysis shows higher $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ associated with \\emph{increased} vulnerability for targeted attacks but \\emph{decreased} vulnerability for shared-bias attacks, and Bayesian calibration corresponds to a sign change.\n\nControlled experiments across 15 models, $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$, 6 attacks, and 2 benchmarks confirm this transition (Figure~\\ref{fig:framework}). Under random faults, panel composition is highly predictive ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$); the predictability collapse under adaptive attack is most pronounced at $K{=}3$. Bayesian calibration corresponds to a sign change in $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction at $K{=}3$ and renders both predictors negligible at $K{=}5$; robustness-first selection dominates diversity-maximizing strategies.\n\nThese findings yield three contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{K-dependent regime transition.} While CJT predicts that panel size affects aggregation quality, we quantify a specific transition: $\\eta_{\\max}$ dominates at $K{=}3$ (bootstrap 5/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 1/12, MDE${=}0.14$); at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the primary predictor (7/12, $\\eta_{\\max}$ 0/12). Beyond this aggregate pattern, we document attack-mechanism-dependent structure (6:6 targeted/shared-bias split) and aggregation modulation (Bayesian reversal), neither predicted by CJT (\\S\\ref{sec:dominance}, \\S\\ref{sec:cross_k}, \\S\\ref{sec:rf_contrast}).\n\\item \\textbf{Attack-type taxonomy.} GAM reveals a 6:6 split by attack mechanism at $K{=}3$ (targeted vs.\\ shared-bias), 11/12 consistent at $K{=}5$. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient positive at aggregate level but direction depends on attack mechanism (\\S\\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).\n\\item \\textbf{Practical defense recommendations.} Robustness-first selection ($\\min \\eta_{\\max}$) dominates diversity-maximizing strategies. Bayesian calibration corresponds to a sign change at $K{=}3$ and renders both predictors negligible at $K{=}5$ (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
        "numLines": 24,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 24
      }
    },

        "content": "\\documentclass[11pt]{article}\n\\usepackage[review]{acl}\n\\usepackage{times}\n\\usepackage{latexsym}\n\\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}\n\\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}\n\\usepackage{microtype}\n\\usepackage{graphicx}\n\\usepackage{amsmath,amssymb,amsthm}\n\\usepackage{booktabs}\n\\usepackage{multirow}\n\\usepackage{xcolor}\n\\usepackage{subcaption}\n\n\\newtheorem{theorem}{Theorem}\n\\newtheorem{definition}{Definition}\n\\newtheorem{proposition}{Proposition}\n\n\\title{The Shifting Bottleneck: How Panel Size Modulates\\\\Adversarial Vulnerability in LLM Judge Panels}\n\n\\author{Anonymous}\n\n\\begin{document}\n\\maketitle\n\n\\begin{abstract}\nMulti-judge LLM evaluation panels are widely assumed to benefit from judge diversity, drawing on fault-tolerance intuitions from distributed systems. We empirically characterize a panel-size-dependent vulnerability regime transition by decomposing panel attack success into individual judge weakness ($\\eta_{\\max}$) and structural diversity ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$) across 15 models, six attack types, two benchmarks, and panel sizes $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ (up to 6,435 panels). At small panels, individual weakness dominates; at larger panels, diversity structure becomes the primary predictor. This aggregate pattern masks attack-mechanism-dependent structure: higher diversity is associated with increased vulnerability for targeted attacks but decreased vulnerability for shared-bias attacks. Aggregation rules further modulate the relationship, with Bayesian calibration corresponding to a sign change in the diversity channel. The defense strategy must match the regime: small panels require screening for individual robustness; larger panels require managing judge independence.\n\\end{abstract}\n\n\\input{introduction}\n\\input{related_work}\n\\input{threat_model}\n\\input{methodology}\n\\input{results}\n\\input{analysis}\n\\input{conclusion}\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur study has four principal limitations. First, the combinatorial panel design introduces non-independence that increases with $K$. We address this with wild cluster bootstrap using $K$-position max-$p$, but the max-$p$ correction grows more conservative at larger $K$, which may partly explain the $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ attenuation from 7/12 at $K{=}5$ to 5/12 at $K{=}7$. Second, both benchmarks use multiple-choice formats whose attack surface (answer permutation, option injection) differs structurally from open-ended evaluation (stylistic manipulation, coherence gaming); the six local models use AWQ INT4 quantization (Table~\\ref{tab:models}). Whether the regime transition holds for open-ended or full-precision settings is unknown. Third, we study only odd $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$ (majority vote threshold $t{=}\\lceil K/2\\rceil$); $K{=}4$ requires supermajority ($t{=}3$), changing the threshold mechanism. The transition threshold $K^*$ ($3 < K^* < 5$) is not precisely identified. Fourth, the transition's strength is model-pool-dependent and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s independent causal contribution remains only partially separable from co-occurring capability variance (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\n\\section*{Code and Data Availability}\nCode, the evaluation framework, and all panel-level results will be released upon acceptance. All evaluation items are sampled from publicly available benchmarks: MMLU \\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} and ARC-Challenge \\citep{clark2018arc}. The nine API-accessed judge models are served through OpenRouter; the six local judges are open-weight models (Qwen 2.5, Llama 3.1, Mistral Large) in AWQ-quantized form. Individual judge scores, panel compositions, and aggregated vulnerability metrics will be included in the release. All experiments use a fixed random seed (42), and all hyperparameters are reported in the methodology section to support reproducibility.\n\n\\input{appendix}\n\\input{appendix_attacks}\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\end{document}\n",
